                1. Introduction

                Processes (aka calculation, analysis modules) and agentic AI (part of Testbed 2026 phase 3, to be tendered later this year) are what set a Digital Twin of the Physical Living environment apart from a GIS or BI environment. Being able to simulate, analyze, predict and calculate are essential components to determine KPI’s so that policy makers can make informed decisions.

                Having open, proven, standards-based, and API-enabled (machine-first) processes allows the process (a calculation or analysis) to be taken up in workflows and automations. However, the human remains in the loop and takes the ultimate decision.

                1.1 Goal of the testbed

                We want to encourage the adoption of uniform interoperable standards for Digital Twins, based on the nLDT architecture (data, processes and visualization, see https://geonovum.github.io/NLDT-Architectuur/). In this Testbed, the focus is on interoperable processes. With these research and adoption topics, we want to create sufficient ‘critical mass’ – enough organizations in an ecosystem that work with the same interoperability arrangements. This will lead to an interoperable, networked system of digital twins for the physical environment.

                The adoption topics use established API specifications, that have a high level of maturity.

                The research topics do not have the same maturity level: here we are identifying (and eliminating) the obstacles for interoperability.

                Geonovum, in line with its mission, is keen to promote the use of standards, drive adoption and foster research; and it seeks to involve the market to do so. The actual questions and issues to be addressed are described in this document as research and adoption topics.

                1.2 Scope

                In this testbed we focus on implementing the OGC API Processes standard. There is no centralized use-case, we request the particpants to implement their own use case.

                1.3 Outcome

                The results of the testbed are intended to contribute to expanding and innovating the Dutch public sector Spatial Data Infrastructure in a direction that considers the possibilities in the market today. Based on the outcome of this testbed we may take steps to make the OGC API Processes standard mandatory within the Dutch public sector, to promote interoperability between digital twins and encourage reuse of processes and calculation models.

                2. Topics

                2.1 Adoption topic #1: Implement and validate an OGC API Processes service

                2.1.1 Goal

                Make an existing calculation module work according to the nLDT reference architecture (https://github.com/Geonovum/NLDT-Architectuur) and have it interoperate with software from other software vendors.

                2.1.2 Description

                Add a full implementation of the OGC API Processes specification to an existing calculation module. With “full” implementation we mean supporting process input & output descriptions, both Sync and Async execution mode, and job management. The implementation must pass the OGC compliance check and pass Geonovum’s OGC-checker with no faults.

                2.1.3 Deliverables

                • A written report of the findings encountered during implementation. Geonovum will provide a Github repository where the report can be added in markdown format. We will act as editors and consolidate all reports into one Respec/HTML document. 
                • The software component developed under this contract must be made available in the standard market offering, at no additional cost.
                • A demo service (with demo data) should be kept available for the public at least until 6 months after the testbed ends.
                • Short demo video’s

                2.1.4 Requirements / standards / Open-Source software

                • Mandatory Standards: https://ogcapi.ogc.org/processes/

                  OGC API Processes v1.0 is the approved version of the API, and v2.0 is a stable draft. We want to encourage using version 2 of the specification.

                • Optional available Open-Source Software (for inspiration and as example code for testing):

                  For OGC API Processes open source client and server implementations are listed in the OGC Github repository: https://github.com/opengeospatial/ogcapi-processes/blob/master/implementations.adoc

                  The above software is listed for convenience; there may be more available.

                2.2 Adoption topic #2: Complete and validate an existing OGC API Processes service

                2.2.1 Goal

                Complete an existing API Processes implementation, make it function within the nLDT ecosystem and have it interoperate with software from other software vendors.

                2.2.2 Description

                Complete an existing implementation of the OGC API Process specification to a full implementation on top of an existing calculation module. With “full” implementation we mean supporting process input & output descriptions, both Sync and Async execution mode and job management. The implementation must pass the OGC compliance check and pass Geonovum’s OGC-checker with no faults.

                2.2.3 Deliverables

                • A written report of the findings encountered during implementation. Geonovum will provide a Github repository where the report can be added in markdown format. We will act as editors and consolidate all reports into one Respec/HTML document. 
                • The software component developed under this contract must be made available in the standard market offering, at no additional cost.
                • A demo service (with demo data) should be kept available for the public at least until 6 months after the testbed ends.
                • Component is ready to do taken up in an AppStore
                • Short demo video’s

                2.2.4 Requirements / standards / Open-Source Software

                • Mandatory Standards: https://ogcapi.ogc.org/processes/

                  OGC API Processes v1.0 is the approved version of the API, and v2.0 is a stable draft. We want to encourage the use of version 2 of the specification.

                • Optional available Open Source Software (for inspiration and as example code for testing):

                  For OGC API Processes open source client and server implementations are listed in the OGC Github repository: https://github.com/opengeospatial/ogcapi-processes/blob/master/implementations.adoc

                  The above software is listed for convenience; there may be more available.

                2.3 Adoption topic #3: Implement and validate OGC API Processes client

                2.3.1 Goal

                Implement an OGC API Processes client, make it function within the nLDT ecosystem and have it interoperate with software from other software vendors.

                2.3.2 Description

                Create a full implementation of an OGC API Process specification client in your visualization software. With “full” implementation we mean supporting process input & output descriptions, both Sync and Async execution mode, and job management.

                2.3.3 Deliverables

                • A written report of the findings encountered during implementation. Geonovum will provide a Github repository where the report can be added in markdown format. We will act as editors and consolidate all reports into one Respec/HTML document. 
                • The software component developed under this contract must be made available in the standard market offering, at no additional cost.
                • A demo client-side service should be kept available for the public at least until 6 months after the testbed ends.
                • Component is ready to do taken up in an AppStore
                • Short demo video’s

                2.3.4 Requirements / standards / Open-Source Software

                • Mandatory Standards: https://ogcapi.ogc.org/processes/

                  OGC API Processes v1.0 is the approved version of the API, and v2.0 is a stable draft. We want to encourage the use of version 2 of the specification.

                • Optional available Open-Source Software (for inspiration and as example code for testing):

                  For OGC API Processes open-source client and server implementations are listed in the OGC Github repository: https://github.com/opengeospatial/ogcapi-processes/blob/master/implementations.adoc

                  The above software is listed for convenience; there may be more available.

                2.4 Research topic #4: App-Store / Recipes

                2.4.1 Goal

                Offer ready-made solutions for real-world problems, expressed as a ‘recipe’: a combination of data-sources (ingredients) and operations in the form of an ordered list of processes (like cooking instructions).

                2.4.2 Description

                We are familiar with the App Store (and Play Store) from our mobile phones (smartphones, smart devices): here we find all kinds of applications ('Apps', applications) that, after installation, work immediately and are integrated with the smartphone ecosystem.

                The Windows and macOS operating systems also have an App Store – where apps are promoted but also organized into categories to make searching easier. Just like on smart devices, installation is flawless and the application works seamlessly within the operating system's ecosystem.

                Payment is easily arranged in both the App Stores of smart devices and those of Windows or macOS – digital payments can be made via all kinds of payment methods.

                App settings are stored in the operating system; when the app is used on another device by the same user, it is immediately recognizable. 

                Digital Twins also operate within their own ecosystem (and together form an ecosystem). Digital Twins are defined using computational modules and an App Store in the context of Digital Twins, it should offer easy-to-install-and-use computational modules.

                Whereas the App Store in smart devices uses the native functions of Windows or macOS, the App Store in Digital Twins uses HTTP as the primary means of communication.

                2.4.2.1 Recipes

                Recipes can be expressed in various ways. OGC API Processes part 2 https://docs.ogc.org/DRAFTS/20-044.html. Common Workflow Language (CWL), a standard that specifies the document schema and execution semantics for composing workflows from components such as command line tools and other workflows. This could be a way to describe recipes. Another proposal for recipe description was created within nLDT. This proposal is not yet standardized and could be used as a basis or inspiration source for experimental implementation. (see appendix B for link to the experimental implementation) A third option for describing recipes is Arazzo, a community-driven open specification within the OpenAPI Initiative. (https://www.openapis.org/arazzo-specification)

                2.4.2.2 Catalogs

                A catalog serves as a central hub where datasets and services are collected, documented, and made accessible. It offers a clear structure in which information is described via metadata, such as title, subjects, origin, and format. This allows users to quickly discover what data is available and how to utilize it, without having to search through separate documents or scattered sources.

                In addition to organizing data, a catalog offers functions for searching, filtering, and comparing services. Users can filter using search terms, categories, or tags to find exactly what is relevant to their application. The system often supports multiple types of services—from open data to internal administrative records—and can process various data types and files, such as tables, geographic data, or API connections.

                An important aspect of such a catalog system is the focus on accessibility and reusability. Through standardization, clear documentation, and reliable connections, it becomes easy to integrate data into analyses, applications, or visualizations. Moreover, the platform encourages collaboration between departments, organizations, or developers by making datasets findable and understandable, which contributes to transparency and innovation.

                2.4.2.3 Metadata

                Metadata consists of descriptive data that helps to find, understand, and correctly interpret datasets and services. They include information about the content, origin, structure, and quality of the data, among other things. By capturing this context, it becomes clear to users exactly what a dataset contains, how current or reliable it is, and in which situations it is suitable for use. In this way, metadata acts as a kind of user manual for data.

                Standards are often used to record metadata in a structured and interchangeable manner. A widely used standard is DCAT, a vocabulary developed to describe services. DCAT defines uniform terms and relationships, making services easier to publish, search, and link between different platforms. Through this standardization, organizations can keep their data descriptions consistent and promote interoperability.

                In addition to improving findability and consistency, metadata also contributes to transparency and governance. They clarify who is responsible for a dataset, which license applies, how it is updated, and what restrictions apply. As a result, users can better assess whether the data is suitable for their purposes, and organizations maintain an overview of their own information management.

                2.4.2.4 Recipe metadata

                DCAT is a mature and widely used standard, and the Netherlands has a standardized DCAT profile: DCAT-AP-NL. However, DCAT-AP-NL is designed to describe datasets and services. It does not currently support metadata for recipes: combinations of data sources and operations that together produce a result.

                For users to find recipes in a catalog, they must be described using metadata, just like datasets and services. How to capture recipe metadata in DCAT-AP-NL is not yet fully defined.

                There are standards extending DCAT to describe properties like Recipes. We are investigating DPROD (Data Product Ontology (DPROD)) and APKG (GitHub - ILIAD-ocean-twin/APKG: Application Package Application Profile · GitHub).

                2.4.3 Deliverables

                • A written report; Geonovum will provide a Github repository where the report can be added in markdown format. We will act as editors and consolidate all reports into one Respec/HTML document. 
                • If any software component is developed, we prefer the code to be made available in an open-source repository and pull requests to existing OS projects in case these are used.
                • The solution should be kept available for the public at least until 6 months after the testbed ends. 
                • Results are input for an AppStore
                • Short demo video’s
                  • A viewer can find the Metadata, bind the recipe and execute the recipe – the results are visualized in the viewer.

                2.4.4 Requirements / standards / Open-Source Software

                • Mandatory Standards:
                  • OGC API Processes
                  • OGC API Records
                  • DCAT-AP-NL

                More on orchestration to be found as a deliverable from Testbed2 (https://github.com/Geonovum/DTaaS-Testbed2/blob/main/deliverables/DTaas2_Orchestration-Avineon_Tensing.pdf).

                OGC API Processes v1.0 is the approved version of the API, and v2.0 is a stable draft. We want to encourage the use of version 2 of the specification.

                • Proposed Standards:
                  • CWL (https://www.commonwl.org)
                  • Arazzo (https://www.openapis.org/arazzo-specification)
                  • Roll-your own (https://github.com/Geonovum/nLDT-Recipe)

                NOTE: We intend to award multiple participants for this research topic. Our aim is to have at least one of each for these Recommended Standards.

                2.5 Research topic #5: Web 3D Context document (export/import)

                2.5.1 Goal

                The nLDT ecosystem knows a lot of visualization components, each having their own strengths and specific capabilities (including AR/VR). The goal of this research topic is the ability to export scene information from a visualization component and the ability to import that scene information in another visualization component.

                2.5.2 Description

                The 3D Context Document states how a specific grouping of one or more maps from one or more map servers can be described in a portable, platform-independent format for storage in a repository or for transmission between clients. This description is known as a Web 3D Context Document,” or simply a “3d Context.” A 3D Context document includes information about the services(s) providing data in the overall view, the 3D viewpoint, time, bounding box and map projection shared by all the data, sufficient operational metadata for Client software to reproduce the 3D viewpoint, and ancillary metadata used to annotate or describe the maps and their provenance for the benefit of human viewers. A 3D Context document is structured using JSON. 

                The work should continue to build on the export-import-scenes: https://geonovum.github.io/DTaaS-Testbed2/#export-import-scenes-6-2-0.

                2.5.3 Deliverables

                • A written report; Geonovum will provide a Github repository where the report can be added in markdown format. We will act as editors an consolidate all reports into one Respec/HTML document. 
                • If any software component is developed, we prefer the code to be made available in an open source repository and pull requests to existing OS projects in case these are used. 
                • The solution should be kept available for the public at least until 6 months after the testbed ends. 
                • Short demo video’s

                2.5.4 Requirements / standards / Open-Source Software

                • Mandatory documents: https://github.com/Geonovum/DTaaS-Testbed2/blob/main/deliverables/Digital_Twin_as_a_Service%E2%80%93Testbed_II-Import_Export_Scenes.pdf (also to be found in the DTaaS-Testbed2 repository, under deliverables).

                  NOTE: We encourage submitting with multiple parties for this topic, each offering a visualization component for use with 3D Context Documents.

                3. Results

                In this chapter only the summaries of the participants are included. The full reports from the participants are included in the Annex.

                3.1 Adoption topic #1: 3DGI

                3DGI added an OGC API – Processes interface to Roofer Online, which reconstructs 3D buildings from point clouds and building footprints. The contribution addresses Topic 1: exposing an existing calculation module through the nLDT architecture and making it usable by other vendors' clients.

                There are two versions of the Roofer Online processes API.

                • v1: A public demo that was developed for the testbed, and it uses fixture data. Live at https://processes.staging.roofer-online.nl/ogcapi.
                  • process catalogue
                  • Swagger UI
                  • OpenAPI document
                • v2: The client-facing live Roofer Online processes API, which requires a Roofer Online account and authentication. Live at https://roofer-online.nl/ogcapi.
                v1: public testbed demo v2: live Roofer Online API
                Purpose Let participants test discovery, execution, job handling and process chaining Run building reconstruction within Roofer Online
                Processing Deterministic fixture results and sample CityJSON/GeoPackage files Point-cloud ingestion, BAG selection, reconstruction and export
                Access Public, no access restriction Public discovery; authenticated execution, jobs, results and artifact downloads
                Processes Separate validation, reconstruction and conversion processes One reconstruction process incorporating preparation and requested exports
                Execution Synchronous and asynchronous Asynchronous only
                Testbed status Tested Not tested because authentication is required

                The reusable components and v1 implementation are public on GitHub, with Apache-2.0 licence: https://github.com/3DGI/roofer-online-processes

                3.2 Adoption topic #1: Clappform

                What we were asked to do

                Topic 1 asks participants to put an OGC API - Processes interface on top of an existing calculation module, following the nLDT reference architecture, and to make it work with software from other vendors. The kickoff named sync and async execution, job management and full compliance with the specification as the bar.

                What we built and validated

                We took a Clappform calculation that existed as a Python notebook: it matches a housing-plan plot (kavel) against BAG buildings and scores how much of each building lies inside the plot. We ported it to Go and exposed it through a Processes server in three forms:

                1. One call, caller supplies the buildings (plot-bag-matching).
                2. One call, the server fetches the buildings from the PDOK BAG WFS (plot-bag-matching-pdok).
                3. Five step processes that a recipe chains together, with every intermediate value visible.

                We started on Processes 1.0 and moved the server to the 2.0 draft halfway through the testbed. We then added the nested-processes class of Part 3, so a client can send the whole five-step recipe as one request. Last, we built a browser console with no dependencies. It reads the process descriptions, generates input forms, follows async jobs, and runs the recipe from a CWL Workflow document, either step by step or as one nested request.

                In parallel we built the same matching as a Python plugin for pygeoapi, which implements Processes 1.0. Section 1.5 describes the plugin and section 5.8 what we fixed before it ran.

                We measured both recipe routes against live PDOK data. Each returned a match_result identical to the single call. 199 automated tests cover the server, among them a test that asserts the chained steps and the single call agree across 7 cases.

                What we found

                1. 2.0 is a major version, so 1.0 clients break. The job status object, the execute request and the sync response shape all changed. A 1.0 client cannot read a 2.0 server. Section 2 lists each change.
                2. A validator can pass a document it never checked. The Geonovum ogc-checker parses OpenAPI 3.0 only. It skipped every rule on our OpenAPI 3.1 document and reported it as valid. A 3.0 copy of the same document produced 21 errors.
                3. The response shape depends on how many outputs you ask for. Zero outputs give 204, one gives the raw value, several give a results document of {"value": …} wrappers. Every client, and every recipe step, needs both code paths.
                4. The protocol lives in headers a browser hides by default. Location, Preference-Applied, Link and OGC-Output-Values-Count are invisible to a browser client unless the server lists them in Access-Control-Expose-Headers.
                5. Part 3 nesting is a tree, not a graph. When one step feeds two others, the nested request contains that step twice and the server runs it twice. The standard has no way to refer to a sibling's output.
                6. Splitting a calculation into steps changes its numbers unless you control rounding and projection. We had to move rounding out of the overlap step and keep geometry repair in lon/lat before the split reproduced the single call exactly.
                7. Neither Processes nor Part 3 carries values between steps for you. Our console does that wiring from the CWL document.
                8. The 1.0 schema allows neither bilingual titles nor type arrays. Our pygeoapi plugin had to fall back to plain strings and nullable. Section 5.8 has the details.

                Our recommendation in one line

                Processes 2.0 is a sound base for a digital-twin calculation layer. Pin down the response shape, add sibling references to Part 3, and make the validators fail loudly on input they cannot read.

                3.3 Adoption topic #2: GeoLabs

                Results at a glance

                Question Answer
                Is the service available through OGC API - Processes? Yes, at https://host1.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/ and https://host2.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/
                Does it pass the official OGC test suite (Part 1: Core 1.0)? Yes: 3 of 3 conformance classes; 53 tests passed, 0 failed, 1 skipped for host1
                Does it pass the Geonovum ogc-checker? Yes: 0 diagnostics of any kind, with the version 1.0.0 rules on host1 and with the version 2.0.0 rules on the second Server Instance host2
                Does it support sync and async execution and job management? Yes, on all three processes on the two server instances
                Does it work with version 2.0 of the standard (draft)? Yes, as far as the tools can tell: the host2 Server Instance passes ogc-checker 2.0.0 with 0 diagnostics; available in the ZOO-Project-DRU Helm chart since version 0.10.5. However, some endpoints are not yet implemented.
                Can other participants’ clients use the service? Partly, they can discover, describe, and run its processes. One client could also display the route on a map; another had issues detecting the input data types/formats, as discussed in Section 5.
                What did the testbed deliver beyond the service? The routing service working as a process accessible from an OGC API - Processes - Part 1: Core Server Instance, three corrections merged into the official OGC schemas, Docker packaging merged into ogc-checker, two Server Instances for testing, and six recommendations (Section 9).

                3.4 Adoption topic #3: Breinstein

                OGC API – Processes lets a client discover, run and monitor computational processes over a Web API. In the Dutch national digital twin architecture (nLDT), a visualisation front end calls computational modules through this standard. Existing conformance testing checks servers from outside the browser, so it cannot show whether a web page can use them. Of the existing open-source client libraries, most do not cover Processes. The one that does reduces each response to its JSON body, so it loses the Location header that asynchronous execution depends on.

                We built a browser client in two layers:

                • a runtime-neutral protocol core, published on npm;
                • a web interface on top of it, which generates input forms and shows results on a map, so a user can run a process without writing any code.

                The client speaks the 2.0 draft of the standard where a service declares it, and falls back to 1.0 otherwise. With it, we tested six deployments: two implementations run by the team throughout the testbed (pygeoapi and ZOO-Project), and three services run by testbed parties. We recorded every point at which a service's behaviour differed from the standard, from its own declarations, or from what a browser allows: 72 filed findings.

                The main results:

                • CORS decides whether a web page can use a service at all, not OGC conformance. Three of the six deployments can be reached directly from a web page. Only one of those three lets a page read the Location header that names a new asynchronous job.
                • Declarations and behaviour diverge in both directions. Capabilities that work are not declared, and capabilities that are declared or listed do not work.
                • Neither team-run implementation validates execute inputs, and in 16 findings a server answers with a success status while doing something other than what was asked.
                • The same request returns differently shaped answers from different implementations.

                We considered a server-side relay to work around these limits, and built one. We decided not to deploy it publicly, for security reasons. Instead we recommend that services configure CORS correctly, and that any access control they need uses a documented scheme that a browser can send. We also propose profile guidance, and identify four gaps in the standard itself.

                3.5 Adoption topic #3: Imagem

                Implement an OGC API Processes client, make it function within the nLDT ecosystem and have it interoperate with software from other software vendors.

                Approach

                For IMAGEM, the approach was to continue to build on the progress made in the previous testbeds (2024 & 2025). In these testbeds, first explorations were done to work with OGC API Processes. Where it all started as a static implementation of OGC API Processes, a more complete implementation of the OGC API Processes (v2) was the aim for this testbed.

                In this approach, some new features of the OGC API Processes were tested and implemented in the Digital Twin client (Planspace Simulator). On one side this meant an attempt to create a user interface based on the input and output descriptions of the OGC API Processes provided during this testbed. On the other hand a Job Management System had to be implemented to track the Async processes of this testbed (In previous testbeds, only Sync processes were part of the Adoption topics).

                Going into detail, the approach can be divided in four steps:

                • Creating a catalogue with OGC API Processes
                • Generating UI elements based on the process definition
                • Creating the Job Management System in the client to manage the Async job executions
                • Visualising the results of the OGC API Processes.

                Client implementation

                The client is the OGC API Explorer in the Planspace Simulator. It is not tied to specific processes: the user selects an OGC API endpoint (Varidian's registry is the default, other endpoints can be added) and the explorer uses the conformance declaration to enable the parts the endpoint supports:

                • Collections (OGC API - Features): browse collections, filter on queryables and add a collection as a layer to the map.
                • Processes (OGC API - Processes): the input form is generated from the process description (JSON Schema), including GeoJSON inputs that can be drawn, uploaded or edited. A process can be executed synchronously or asynchronously, depending on its jobControlOptions.
                • Jobs: asynchronous executions are listed and polled until they finish, with status, progress and timestamps. Jobs can be dismissed when the endpoint supports it, and the results of a finished job can be opened.
                • Results: outputs are rendered read-only from the output schema. GeoJSON, GeoTIFF and feature collection outputs can be added to the map as a layer.

                3.6 Research topic #4: Collaboral

                Collaborall investigated how a recipe — a combination of data sources and an ordered list of processes — can be described, found and executed in a national digital-twin App Store, using OGC API Processes, OGC API Records and DCAT-AP-NL. We built the answer on ANT, our existing workflow platform, because that is the realistic case: an organisation adopts these standards on the platform it already runs.

                Our main findings and advice:

                1. A recipe needs its own place in DCAT-AP-NL. It is neither a dataset nor a data service. We propose a small extension — two classes and six properties — that catalogues a recipe beside the datasets and services it uses, so a catalogue can answer which recipes use this service or this dataset. The extension validates against the published DCAT-AP 3.0 and DCAT-AP-NL 3.0 shapes.
                2. A process and a recipe differ in visibility, not in structure. A process is a black box and is catalogued as an ordinary dcat:DataService; a recipe is a transparent composition whose steps can be inspected.
                3. Arazzo is not enough for recipes. It describes a sequence of API calls and nothing else of a workflow. We moved to the Open Workflow Specification (OWS); another awardee independently left Arazzo as well. We advise Geonovum to choose one recipe language and the subset of it a recipe may use.
                4. No standard and no platform carries a value from one step to the next. That wiring is described by the recipe language and has to be executed by whoever runs the recipe.
                5. A catalogue that conforms to the profile can still be rejected by a harvester. The harvester behind data.europa.eu refuses our valid catalogue because of the links that make a recipe a recipe. An extension needs to be tested against real harvesters, not only against shapes.
                6. Several recipes ask for the same plan in different shapes. A shared, published concept set for a scenario — an area and a building programme — would let one input serve many recipes. We built a proof of concept on an existing OGC keyword.

                3.7 Research topic #4: FutureInsight

                We built a working, end-to-end App Store for recipes: a catalogue (OGC API Records over CKAN), a Cookbook Registry, a Cook that is itself an OGC API Process, and a web client. Recipes are written in CWL v1.2 and executed against our own processes and external testbed processes (roofer, Geolabs ZOO-Project, Varidian).

                Our main conclusion: CWL works as a recipe format, but not unextended. CWL describes ingredients, order and wiring well. It cannot say where a step's OGC API Process lives, which alternative processes a hub may use, or describe process inputs as precisely as JSON Schema does. We added three small extension fields in a declared nldt: namespace to close those gaps; with them, all five bundled recipes pass the reference validator, cwltool --validate.

                Other key findings:

                • CWL's abstract Operation step maps one-to-one onto a remote OGC API Process.
                • CWL's own File + format cleanly separates data ingredients from parameters, and maps onto OGC by-reference (href) inputs.
                • OGC API Processes descriptions do not say machine-readably which inputs are datasets, so the recipe must declare it.
                • Asynchronous execution with job polling is mandatory in practice for long-running processes.
                • DCAT-AP-NL has no recipe concept yet; proposing the extension is the main open research item.
                • Writing CWL by hand is hard for non-specialists; a guided editor is essential.
                • Every part of the stack works from one plan: the Cookbook Registry turns a recipe into a DAG, and the editor, catalogue, Wizard, Status tab and Cook all ask it for that plan instead of reading the CWL themselves.
                • An OGC API Process description already carries most of what a user needs to fill in a parameter (title, description, default, limits); the Wizard reads it when it shows the form, so recipes need not copy it.

                3.8 Research topic #4: Varidian

                Varidian started in May 2026 with creating a registry. The initial goal of this registry was to provide a standardized interface for connecting with multiple data sources. All communication would go through the registry and data source-specific logic only has to exist inside this registry and its connected microservices instead of inside every individual client. Quickly after the initial working version, the need arose to also be able to execute processes using a standardized interface. Both registry implementations for these features were based on the OGC Features and OGC Processes API’s; and after finalizing the initial implementation for the processes interface in the registry the need for chaining processes became abundantly clear. This is when the overlap between the registry’s roadmap and Research topic #4: App-Store / Recipes of the testbed was observed. We are building a catalog of collections and processes to fill the gaps in information and analysis that our own solutions run into. This testbed lets us find out whether recipes can be added to this catalog, where recipes are a combination of data-sources (ingredients) and operations in the form of an ordered list of processes (like cooking instructions. An important aspect of experiencing whether recipes fit in our catalog is to determine whether stating requirements about a recipe up front makes it possible to say something about the quality of the results that come out. Without reliable outcome, the selection of the most applicable or trustworthy recipe comes down to luck or expertise. Since we want to be able to use the results as information source for our own solutions, needing either luck or expertise means it is not ready to be integrated. By researching which metadata is required to enrich both the source data and the process’s inputs and outputs we hope to find a way to make a recipe’s results readable and trustworthy, which is exactly the focus of this research topic.

                3.9 Research topic #5: ThePeopleGroup

                The People Group implemented an exporter and an importer for Scene Context JSON (SCX) in its DigitalTwin platform and opened the exported scenes side by side in CesiumJS and ArcGIS. This report documents what we found, with the documents, code, measurements and service responses each finding is based on, and translates the findings into proposals for the SCX specification.

                The main conclusions:

                • The camera position travels, the lens does not. Position, heading and tilt were reproduced exactly in three engines. The visible area differed by almost a factor of two because each engine uses a different default and definition of the field of view. We added fov and propose projection type and clipping planes (1.2).
                • The nested interval structure is hard to implement and easy to get wrong. It required workarounds in a typed environment and our own exporter produced output that does not validate. We propose a simple { start, end } object (1.1).
                • A layer reference must be complete and typed unambiguously. An incomplete WMS URL produced two different pictures from one document, and independently written exporter and importer code already disagreed on format strings. We propose that url is the complete request and a closed type/format table (1.4, 1.5).
                • The same 3D Tiles give different results per viewer. All 1309 building tiles of one dataset contain invalid glTF that Cesium-based viewers accept and ArcGIS silently does not draw; heights stored as NAP but declared ellipsoidal cause a vertical offset of about 41 m in ArcGIS; instanced tree tiles are not supported by ArcGIS at all. We propose per-layer metadata for tile version, content type and vertical datum (1.6, 1.9).
                • Simulation results cannot be exchanged yet. 21 of the 28 resources in the test scene, including every time-dependent simulation result, have no stable URL another viewer can request. The 4D part of the scene therefore cannot be exported today (2.3).
                • Results between implementations are only comparable with a shared status vocabulary per layer (1.7).

                3.10 Research topic #5: uCrowds

                Crowd simulation helps event organizers evaluate pedestrian movement and identify potential safety risks before an event takes place. Temporary event infrastructure, such as stages, barriers, entrances, and exits, is typically designed in CAD applications. Crowd simulation modelers often need to manually recreate walkable areas, obstacles, and other relevant features from these designs. This process is time-consuming, introduces opportunities for errors, and must be repeated when event designs change.

                This research investigates how SCX, a lightweight format for exchanging scene information between applications, can be extended to support crowd simulation. The extension presented in this report adds semantic information to geographic data, allowing simulation applications to distinguish between features such as walkable areas, obstacles, entrances, exits, and queues.

                The research was carried out by uCrowds and AniVizzion. AniVizzion implemented the export workflow in SCAD Re-Event, while uCrowds implemented the import workflow in SimCrowds. The demonstration showed that event designs can be transferred directly from CAD into SimCrowds, where the relevant simulation objects and a navigation mesh are created without manual redrawing.

                The workflow automates construction of the simulation environment, although users must still connect simulation objects into pedestrian flows and configure simulation parameters. Further research could improve interoperability through shared semantic definitions, investigate applications in other simulation domains, and explore closer integration between CAD design and simulation.

                A. Topic 1: 3DGI

                The reusable components and v1 implementation are public on GitHub, with Apache-2.0 licence: https://github.com/3DGI/roofer-online-processes

                A.2 Roofer Online

                Roofer Online is a web service for creating 3D building models from LAS/LAZ point clouds and BAG building polygons. Users upload their point clouds, select buildings from BAG data synchronised with Kadaster, and configure reconstruction. Processing runs on the server, so users do not need to install reconstruction software. Models can be viewed in 3D, downloaded or published as 3D Tiles. The main goal of Roofer Online is to enable Dutch municipalities and provinces to generate up-to-date 3D building models from their local point clouds.

                The service uses the open-source Roofer reconstruction software, and the same parameters that are used for producing the 3DBAG.

                Roofer Online has a React frontend, a FastAPI backend and a Dagster processing layer. The frontend uses the service's existing internal application API to manage data, start jobs, poll their status and retrieve results. A separate authentication handler manages user sign-in. The API uses bearer-token authentication with JWT tokens and checks user roles and ownership to control access to processes and assets. Dagster orchestrates point-cloud preparation, BAG synchronisation, reconstruction, format conversion and database export, managing dependencies between processing steps. The backend submits jobs through Dagster's GraphQL API and stores process records with their Dagster run identifiers in PostgreSQL. When the frontend polls an active process, the backend queries Dagster, updates the stored process and asset statuses, and records output metadata when processing completes. This job lifecycle existed before the OGC API interface was added.

                A central question for us was how to layer an OGC API – Processes interface over the existing backend API so that external clients could invoke the processes already available through the UI. The aim was to provide direct, controlled access for automation and integration with other software, while reusing Roofer Online's authentication, ownership checks and job lifecycle.

                The Testbed proposal identified four Roofer Online capabilities to expose as OGC API processes:

                Proposed process Role in Roofer Online
                validate_point_cloud Checks whether a point cloud is suitable for reconstruction, including its spatial extent, classification and density.
                reconstruct_buildings Reconstructs 3D buildings from the selected BAG polygons and point clouds, producing CityJSON models.
                convert_format Converts CityJSON models to OBJ, GeoPackage or 3D Tiles for use in other software or publication.
                export_to_3dcitydb Exports CityJSON models to a 3DCityDB database. The 3DCityDB export process was not implemented in the testbed.

                Figure 1. Select an uploaded point cloud after validation.

                Figure 2. Select BAG buildings within the point-cloud extent, outlined in green.

                Figure 3. Review the inputs and reconstruction settings before starting the job.

                Figure 4. Access completed models and choose a download or export format.

                A.3 Implementation

                The API accepts LAS/LAZ URLs, a BAG building selection and processing options as JSON, and returns metadata and download links for building models. We implemented two versions:

                • v1 demo: three processes for validation, reconstruction and conversion, using fixed sample data. This version was tested during the testbed.
                • v2 live service: one asynchronous process combining preparation, reconstruction and export of client data. It requires JWT bearer-token authentication and was not tested during the testbed.

                Both versions reuse the ogc-processes protocol layer. The live integration adds process definitions from roofer-processes-contracts and adapters to Roofer Online's existing authentication, Dagster workflows and persistent job storage.

                A.3.1 Data

                Clients submit inputs in the inputs object of a JSON body to POST /ogcapi/processes/{processID}/execution, with Content-Type: application/json.

                • Point clouds: one or more LAS/LAZ files, supplied as HTTP(S) URL objects, for example {"kind": "url", "url": "https://data.example/survey.laz"}. The files are hosted by the client or a data provider, their contents are not embedded in the request, because point cloud data is usually very large. The live service downloads the data from the provided URLs. Their horizontal coordinates must align with the BAG footprints in EPSG:28992, the service does not reproject them.
                • Building selection: BAG building identifiers (identificatie attribute), for example {"kind": "buildings", "building_ids": ["0000000000000001"]}, or a WKT Polygon/MultiPolygon with "kind": "area" and "crs": "EPSG:28992". Roofer Online obtains the corresponding BAG footprints, clients supply the selection.
                • Settings and formats: optional reconstruction settings in config, for example {"ceil-point-density": 10}, and an optional model name. The formats array selects downloads: v1 supports cityjson and gpkg, v2 also supports obj, cityjson_terrain and 3dtiles, and defaults to CityJSON.
                • Validation results: the v1 validation process returns a JSON readiness report with per-source readiness, CRS, bounds, point count and density. These are sample values in the demo.
                • Model outputs: reconstruction and conversion return JSON metadata and download links in artifacts, keyed by format. Each link has an href and media type, clients download the files with HTTP GET. The live v2 outputs are ZIP archives (application/zip), and downloads require the client's JWT bearer token. For asynchronous execution, clients retrieve the result document from GET /ogcapi/jobs/{jobID}/results after the job succeeds.

                For example, a v2 reconstruction request combines the source, selection and output formats in one JSON body. Send it to POST /ogcapi/processes/roofer:reconstruct_buildings:v2/execution with Prefer: respond-async and Authorization: Bearer <JWT>:

                {
  "inputs": {
    "point_clouds": [{"kind": "url", "url": "https://data.example/survey.laz"}],
    "bag": {"kind": "buildings", "building_ids": ["0000000000000001"]},
    "formats": ["cityjson", "gpkg"]
  }
}

                A.3.2 Processes

                A.3.2.1 v1 - demo

                The public demo exposes three Roofer Online processes:

                Process Input Result passed to the next step
                roofer:validate_point_cloud:v1 Remote LAS/LAZ URL objects Readiness report containing point-cloud IDs
                roofer:reconstruct_buildings:v1 Point-cloud IDs and a BAG selection Model ID and artifact links
                roofer:convert_format:v1 Model ID and requested CityJSON/GeoPackage formats Artifact links for the selected formats

                An additional echo process supports the OGC compliance suite. The proposed 3DCityDB export (export_to_3dcitydb) was not implemented, because it would require authentication with an external 3DCityDB service this complexity is outside the scope of the testbed.

                Clients can discover input/output schemas, execute synchronously or request asynchronous execution with Prefer: respond-async, follow the returned job URL, poll status, list jobs and retrieve results. The demo does not download the supplied point clouds or run reconstruction: it returns fixed sample files. Jobs and generated asset registrations are held in memory and disappear on restart.

                A.3.2.2 v2 - live Roofer Online

                The live v2 API exposes one process, roofer:reconstruct_buildings:v2, which combines point-cloud preparation, reconstruction and export. Clients submit remote LAS/LAZ URLs, a BAG building or area selection, optional reconstruction settings and requested output formats in one request, instead of passing asset IDs between the three v1 processes. The service downloads and processes the supplied data through Roofer Online's workflows and supports CityJSON, GeoPackage, OBJ, CityJSON with terrain and 3D Tiles outputs. Execution is asynchronous only, and jobs persist across restarts. Discovery remains public; execution, job monitoring, results and artefact downloads require JWT bearer-token authentication and follow Roofer Online's access rules. This authenticated v2 workflow was not tested during the testbed.

                A.3.3 Architecture

                The architecture separates OGC protocol handling, Roofer Online process definitions and integration with the live service. This allowed standards testing against the demo while keeping the protocol code independent of Roofer Online's database and Dagster.

                The ogc-processes package implements the following resources from OGC API – Processes Part 1: Core, plus the Job list extension. Paths are relative to /ogcapi.

                Purpose OGC resource and operation
                Discover available processes and their input/output definitions GET /processes and GET /processes/{processID}
                Submit inputs to a process POST /processes/{processID}/execution
                List jobs and check an execution's status GET /jobs and GET /jobs/{jobID}
                Retrieve the outputs of a completed job GET /jobs/{jobID}/results

                The package parses execution requests, selects synchronous or asynchronous execution from the Prefer header, and constructs responses with the required status codes, headers, links and output structure. For example, an asynchronous submission returns HTTP 201 with a job description and a Location header pointing to the job's status URL.

                To connect these operations to a host application, the create_app FastAPI application factory receives five adapters: Python objects implementing the interfaces below. This division allows us to separate the external OGC API from the internal interfaces.

                Adapter Responsibility
                ProcessCatalog Supplies available processes, input/output schemas and supported execution modes.
                ProcessContractValidator Checks process-specific inputs before submission and backend outputs before returning them to clients.
                Authenticator Resolves the caller's identity using the host's authentication mechanism.
                ExecutionBackend Submits work to the host's processing engine and retrieves execution status and outputs.
                JobStore Stores each OGC job with its owner and backend execution identifier, and retrieves jobs for that owner.

                Dagster tracks workflow runs, the JobStore tracks the jobs exposed through the OGC API. It retains a public job ID, its owner and a mapping to the backend execution. One OGC job can span several Dagster runs, such as point-cloud preparation followed by reconstruction, while clients follow one job and list only their own jobs. The execution adapter obtains processing status and results from the backend. JobStore does not require a separate database: its implementation can use existing Roofer Online records where they provide the required information. The demo stores jobs in memory, the live integration persists them in PostgreSQL.

                Separating these responsibilities lets a host change its process definitions, authentication, workflow engine or storage without changing the HTTP implementation. In the demo, the adapters use predefined sample data, simulated execution, unverified demo identities and in-memory job storage. These are the “fixture adapters”: they let clients exercise the OGC interface without running against the live Roofer Online service.

                The roofer-processes-contracts package supplies the live v2 process catalogue and validator using the interfaces and response models defined by ogc-processes. Its Pydantic models define both the published input/output schemas and runtime validation rules. Keeping these definitions separate from the database and workflow engine lets the production contract be tested without running Roofer Online.

                RooferCatalog and RooferContractValidator fill the ProcessCatalog and ProcessContractValidator slots described above. For roofer:reconstruct_buildings:v2, the catalogue supplies the process description returned by GET /processes/{processID}. On an execution request, the validator checks the inputs against ReconstructionInputs and passes normalised values to the execution backend, invalid inputs are rejected before submission. When results are retrieved, it checks the backend's building_model output against BuildingModel before the OGC layer returns it to the client. The remaining three adapters connect this contract to Roofer Online's authentication, processing and storage.

                Roofer Online installs both packages and mounts the OGC application at /ogcapi alongside its existing API. It supplies adapters for JWT authentication, execution and persistent job storage. The execution adapter calls shared Python application services behind the existing API, reusing ownership checks, asset management and Dagster workflows. The job store links each public OGC job to its owner and backend execution, the execution adapter translates backend status and results into OGC responses. External clients can therefore submit and monitor work through the standard interface while the existing backend retains control of access and processing.

                Illustrative host integration, where jwt_authenticator, execution_backend and job_store are Roofer Online's adapters:

                from ogc_processes.router import create_app
from roofer_processes_contracts.catalog import RooferCatalog, RooferContractValidator

app.mount("/ogcapi", create_app(
    catalog=RooferCatalog(),
    validator=RooferContractValidator(),
    authenticator=jwt_authenticator,
    backend=execution_backend,
    store=job_store,
))

                A.3.4 Why not pygeoapi?

                We wanted to separate OGC protocol handling from process definitions and the host application's authentication, storage and orchestration. This let us test the protocol layer with demo adapters, then reuse it with the live process contracts and Roofer Online's existing services. The same interfaces connect both versions without coupling the OGC implementation to Dagster or Roofer Online's database.

                pygeoapi supports custom process plugins and replaceable job managers, so a Dagster integration would also be possible. We chose our own adapter interfaces because they directly express the separation we needed, while allowing the OGC routes to call the application services behind our existing FastAPI API and reuse its authentication and ownership rules. We judged the required protocol surface: discovery, execution, job monitoring and results, are manageable to implement.

                A.4 Conformance

                Conformance was tested on the v1 demo API.

                A.4.1 ogc-checker

                We ran @geonovum/ogc-checker 1.3.1 as a local package, using the ruleset for the ogc-api-processes 2.0.0:

                npm exec -- ogc-checker validate \
  --standard ogc-api-processes --version 2.0.0 \
  --input "https://processes.staging.roofer-online.nl/ogcapi/openapi.json" \
  --fail-on warn

                The run returned:
