                A.4.2 OGC TEAM Engine

                We used TEAM Engine 6.0.0-RC2 with version 1.3 of the OGC API – Processes Executable Test Suite (ETS), packaged in the Docker image ogccite/ets-ogcapi-processes10:1.3-teamengine-6.0.0-RC2. The compliance profile is OGC API – Processes – Part 1: Core, version 1.0.0 (OGC 18-062r2), exposed as suite ogcapi-processes-1.0 (suite documentation).

                We run TEAM Engine in a local container and start validation through its REST API with a GET request to /teamengine/rest/suites/ogcapi-processes-1.0/run, using these parameters:

                • iut=https://processes.staging.roofer-online.nl/ogcapi: the deployed v1 demo API under test.
                • echoprocessid=echo: the process used for execution tests.

                No additional test-selection parameters are supplied. The runner treats failed or skipped tests as a failure. The run completed with 54 passed, 0 failed and 0 skipped:

                OGC API - Processes ETS passed all 54 tests without skips. Report: artifacts/ogc-processes-ets.xml

                A.5 Interoperability

                A.5.1 Future Insight client

                During the first Plugfest, Future Insight successfully integrated the v1 demo API into their recipe orchestrator. The implementation succeeds in calling the v1 processes in order and passing the required input/output from one process to the next.

                A.5.2 IMAGEM Planspace

                The IMAGEM Planspace client lists Roofer Online as one of the available OGC Processes API-s, with the three processes in the v1 and its input fields. The screenshot below shows the reconstruction process.

                A.6 Findings

                • An OGC interface can reuse an existing service backend. Roofer Online exposes its processing through standard endpoints while retaining its FastAPI application services, JWT authentication, ownership checks and Dagster workflows.

                • The protocol implementation was reused across different process interfaces. The v1 demo and live v2 service share HTTP handling and OGC response construction while supplying different process definitions and backend adapters. Testing v1 exercised the shared protocol code, but it did not validate v2’s OGC-conformance or authenticated workflow.

                • A public process need not correspond to one internal workflow step. The v1 demo exposes validation, reconstruction and conversion separately. The live v2 process combines these steps, allowing clients to submit source data and requested outputs without managing intermediate assets.

                • OGC jobs need a stable identity across backend workflows. One client request can involve several Dagster runs. The job store maintains the public job identifier, ownership and execution mapping so clients can monitor the operation as one job.

                • Authenticated interoperability remains to be tested. The live v2 API was excluded from testbed testing because it requires authentication. Further testing should cover authenticated submission, polling and downloads with participant clients and real input data.

                B. Topic 1: Clappform

                Implementing OGC API - Processes 2.0 on top of a calculation module

                Geonovum DTaaS Testbed 2026, phase 2, adoption topic 1: implementation report

                Clappform B.V. | Version 1.0 (draft) | 20 November 2026

                B.1 Document control

                Item Detail
                Report title Implementing OGC API - Processes 2.0 on top of a calculation module
                Programme Geonovum DTaaS Testbed 2026, phase 2, adoption topic 1
                Implementing party Clappform B.V.
                Version 1.0 (draft)
                Status Draft for review
                Date 20 November 2026
                Source code https://github.com/ClappFormOrg/ogc-api-processes (EUPL-1.2)
                Public endpoint https://api.ogc-digital-twin.clappform.com (Part 1 classes; Part 3 nested-processes not yet deployed there)

                Table 1. Document control.

                Standards in scope

                Standard Version we implement Status of the standard
                OGC API - Processes - Part 1: Core 2.0 draft (18-062r3) in the Go server; 1.0 (18-062r2) in the pygeoapi plugin Draft (2.0), adopted (1.0)
                OGC API - Processes - Part 3: Workflows and Chaining nested-processes class only (21-009) Candidate SWG draft, conformance URIs at version 0.0
                OpenAPI 3.0.3 for /api Adopted
                Common Workflow Language v1.2 Workflow, as the recipe definition in our console Adopted

                Table 2. Standards we worked against.

                A note on terminology. OGC API - Processes, shortened to Processes in this report, is an OGC standard for running a calculation over HTTP. A server lists its processes, describes each process's inputs and outputs with JSON Schema, and runs a process on request. A run is either synchronous (the answer comes back in the response) or asynchronous (the server creates a job that the client polls). A recipe is a chain of processes where the outputs of one step feed the inputs of the next. BAG is the Dutch national register of buildings and addresses, which PDOK publishes as open data.

                B.2 Executive summary

                B.2.1 What we were asked to do

                Topic 1 asks participants to put an OGC API - Processes interface on top of an existing calculation module, following the nLDT reference architecture, and to make it work with software from other vendors. The kickoff named sync and async execution, job management and full compliance with the specification as the bar.

                B.2.2 What we built and validated

                We took a Clappform calculation that existed as a Python notebook: it matches a housing-plan plot (kavel) against BAG buildings and scores how much of each building lies inside the plot. We ported it to Go and exposed it through a Processes server in three forms:

                1. One call, caller supplies the buildings (plot-bag-matching).
                2. One call, the server fetches the buildings from the PDOK BAG WFS (plot-bag-matching-pdok).
                3. Five step processes that a recipe chains together, with every intermediate value visible.

                We started on Processes 1.0 and moved the server to the 2.0 draft halfway through the testbed. We then added the nested-processes class of Part 3, so a client can send the whole five-step recipe as one request. Last, we built a browser console with no dependencies. It reads the process descriptions, generates input forms, follows async jobs, and runs the recipe from a CWL Workflow document, either step by step or as one nested request.

                In parallel we built the same matching as a Python plugin for pygeoapi, which implements Processes 1.0. Section 1.5 describes the plugin and section 5.8 what we fixed before it ran.

                We measured both recipe routes against live PDOK data. Each returned a match_result identical to the single call. 199 automated tests cover the server, among them a test that asserts the chained steps and the single call agree across 7 cases.

                B.2.3 What we found

                1. 2.0 is a major version, so 1.0 clients break. The job status object, the execute request and the sync response shape all changed. A 1.0 client cannot read a 2.0 server. Section 2 lists each change.
                2. A validator can pass a document it never checked. The Geonovum ogc-checker parses OpenAPI 3.0 only. It skipped every rule on our OpenAPI 3.1 document and reported it as valid. A 3.0 copy of the same document produced 21 errors.
                3. The response shape depends on how many outputs you ask for. Zero outputs give 204, one gives the raw value, several give a results document of {"value": …} wrappers. Every client, and every recipe step, needs both code paths.
                4. The protocol lives in headers a browser hides by default. Location, Preference-Applied, Link and OGC-Output-Values-Count are invisible to a browser client unless the server lists them in Access-Control-Expose-Headers.
                5. Part 3 nesting is a tree, not a graph. When one step feeds two others, the nested request contains that step twice and the server runs it twice. The standard has no way to refer to a sibling's output.
                6. Splitting a calculation into steps changes its numbers unless you control rounding and projection. We had to move rounding out of the overlap step and keep geometry repair in lon/lat before the split reproduced the single call exactly.
                7. Neither Processes nor Part 3 carries values between steps for you. Our console does that wiring from the CWL document.
                8. The 1.0 schema allows neither bilingual titles nor type arrays. Our pygeoapi plugin had to fall back to plain strings and nullable. Section 5.8 has the details.

                B.2.4 Our recommendation in one line

                Processes 2.0 is a sound base for a digital-twin calculation layer. Pin down the response shape, add sibling references to Part 3, and make the validators fail loudly on input they cannot read.

                B.3 1. What we built

                B.3.1 1.1 Shape of the system

                 Browser console              OGC API - Processes server (Go)                  Open data
 (web/index.html)       ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                        │ /  /conformance  /api (OpenAPI 3.0.3)        │
 Processes tab ───────► │ /processes  /processes/{id}                  │
 Jobs tab      ───────► │ /processes/{id}/execution ── Prefer ──┐      │
 Recipe tab (CWL) ────► │   │  nested-processes resolver (Part 3) │      │
 Service tab   ───────► │   ▼                                     ▼      │
                        │ process registry ──► sync response   job store │
                        │   ├ plot-bag-matching            (in memory, │
                        │   ├ plot-bag-matching-pdok ──────┐  TTL 48 h)│
                        │   └ 5 step processes ────────────┤           │
                        │ /jobs  /jobs/{id}  /jobs/{id}/results[/{out}]│
                        └──────────────────────────────────┼───────────┘
                                                           ▼
                                              PDOK BAG WFS 2.0 (bag:pand)

                Figure 1. Components.

                The server ships as a container with a docker compose file. The console is a single HTML page that you open from disk or from any static host.

                B.3.2 1.2 The calculation module

                The module answers one question for a municipality's housing plan: which BAG buildings stand on this plot, and is the plot built?

                1. Take the plot polygon in WGS84 and repair it if it intersects itself.
                2. Take the BAG buildings around it (bag:pand polygons).
                3. Project both to RD New (EPSG:28992) and compute, per building, the share of its area inside the plot.
                4. Drop buildings below min_overlap_ratio (default 0.05). Classify the rest as full (at or above full_match_threshold, default 0.95) or partial (0.05 ≤ overlap ratio < 0.95).
                5. Flag the plot when it has no building inside (geen_bag_object_binnen_kavel) or only partial matches (alleen_partiele_match).

                We kept the notebook's inputs, outputs, defaults and flag reasons, so existing consumers of the notebook output read the process output unchanged.

                B.3.3 1.3 Process catalogue

                Process Inputs Outputs Calls PDOK
                plot-bag-matching plot, bag_buildings, min_overlap_ratio and full_match_threshold match_result no
                plot-bag-matching-pdok plot, min_overlap_ratio and full_match_threshold match_result, bag_query yes
                1 plot-geometry-prepare geometry geometry, geometry_report no
                2 plot-bbox geometry bbox no
                3 bag-panden-fetch bbox bag_buildings, bag_query yes
                4 plot-bag-overlap geometry, bag_buildings, min_overlap_ratio overlaps, skipped no
                5 bag-match-classify overlaps, plan_dataid, full_match_threshold match_result no

                Table 3. The plot to BAG processes. All support sync and async execution and dismissal.

                bag_query reports the WFS URL, the bounding box, the feature count, the number of requests and a truncated flag. It exists because an empty match_result has two causes: there is no building on the plot, or the WFS returned nothing. Without bag_query a client cannot tell them apart.

                B.3.4 1.4 The web console

                Figure 2. Processes tab: the input form is generated from the process description.

                The console has four tabs:

                • Processes lists the processes, generates a form from each input's JSON Schema, lets you pick the outputs and the Prefer mode, and shows the raw request and response with the OGC headers.

                • Jobs filters the job list by process, status and time, fetches results and dismisses jobs.

                  Figure 3. Jobs tab.

                • Recipe reads a CWL Workflow document, draws the flow from it, and runs it. Each task lights up as it runs, shows its own timing, and opens to show the exact request and response. Compare runs the single-call process on the same plot and diffs the two results.

                  Figure 4. Recipe tab. The diagram and the run order come from the CWL document.

                • Service shows the landing page, the conformance classes and the API definition.

                A recorded walkthrough is in plot-bag-matching-process.mp4.

                B.3.5 1.5 A second implementation: a pygeoapi plugin

                We also turned the notebook into a Python plugin for pygeoapi, which implements Processes 1.0.

                In the notebooks, the matching logic depended on the Clappform platform and on live PDOK requests. We first cut it loose from both. The result is a standalone function behind a fixed execute() interface:

                • Input: the plot geometry and the BAG buildings around it. The caller supplies the buildings, as with plot-bag-matching.
                • Output: the matched BAG IDs, each with an overlap score, and a flag for plots without a full match.

                The overlap score is the share of each building inside the plot, as in section 1.2. We chose this over intersection over union. With intersection over union, the plot's own area dilutes the score, so a small building on a large plot falls far short of a full match. With our measure it scores 1.0.

                B.4 2. The conversion from 1.0 to 2.0

                We built the first version against Processes 1.0 (18-062r2) in March 2026. On 30 September we moved it to the 2.0 draft (18-062r3) for the core, ogc-process-description, json, job-list, callback and dismiss classes.

                B.4.1 2.1 What changed for a client

                Area 1.0 2.0 draft
                Job status jobID, type: "process", bare processID id, processingEntityType: "ogc-api-processes", absolute process URL as processID
                Execute request response: raw | document, transmissionMode Both removed. The number of requested outputs decides the shape
                Sync response Chosen by response 204 for no outputs, the raw value for one, a results document plus a Link: …ogc-results; rel="profile" for several
                Conformance URIs http://…/1.0/conf/… https://…/2.0/conf/…

                Table 4. Changes in the standard that break a 1.0 client.

                OGC keeps minor versions backwards compatible and saves breaking changes for a major version, so 2.0 does not have to work with 1.0 clients. 2.0 also adds features that break nothing, such as /jobs/{id}/results/{outputId} with an OGC-Output-Values-Count header, and a DELETE that removes a finished job.

                We had one client, our own console, so we changed both at once. A provider with external consumers needs to run both versions side by side, on separate base URLs, for a migration period.

                The move also made us fix 2 deviations in our own 1.0 server, which were not changes in the standard:

                • A failed sync run returned 200 with an error output. It now returns 400 for invalid input, 502 for an upstream failure and 500 otherwise, as application/problem+json.
                • A Prefer mode the process could not honour returned 501. 1.0 already asked the server to fall back to the mode the process supports. Ours now does, and omits Preference-Applied.

                B.4.2 2.2 What changed in the server

                • Inputs are validated against their full JSON Schema. 1.0 checked the top-level type. 2.0 makes the description the contract, so we validate nested required, properties, items, enum, minimum/maximum, oneOf and type arrays. Qualified values ({"value": …, "mediaType": …}) are unwrapped first.
                • Every input declares valuePassing: ["byValue"] and an explicit minOccurs. We do not support {"href": …} inputs, and we reject them with a clear message instead of fetching the URL.
                • Process functions return (outputs, error). The error carries the HTTP status, so a PDOK outage reaches the client as 502 and bad input as 400.
                • Callbacks. With a subscriber, the server posts the job status to inProgressUri and failedUri, and the results document to successUri, with a 30 second timeout.
                • /api serves an OpenAPI 3.0.3 document whose bodies reference the official 2.0 schemas.

                B.4.3 2.3 Validation

                We validated with the Geonovum ogc-checker, rule set ogc-api-processes 2.0.0, against our /api:

                npx @geonovum/ogc-checker@latest validate --standard ogc-api-processes --version 2.0.0 \
  --input http://localhost:8080/api

                On 30 September the checker reported no requirement failures. Its remaining invalid-ref errors come from broken $refs inside the draft's own processes-core/value.yaml and valueNoObject.yaml (to cql2.yaml, geometryGeoJSON.yaml and missing CQL2 definitions). Section 5.1 describes how we got there.

                B.5 3. From one process to a recipe

                B.5.1 3.1 Five steps that reproduce the single call exactly

                We split plot-bag-matching-pdok into five step processes (table 3). Chaining all five reproduces plot-bag-matching-pdok. Skipping steps 2 and 3 and supplying bag_buildings reproduces plot-bag-matching. Both single-call processes now run on the same functions as the steps, so the two routes cannot drift apart.

                The split pays for itself in two places:

                • Threshold sweeps. Step 4 computes the overlaps once. Step 5 reclassifies them at any full_match_threshold in 3 to 4 ms, without a PDOK request or any geometry work.
                • Reuse. Step 3 fetches the BAG buildings for an area once, and several plots in that area reuse them.
                full_match_threshold Full matches Partial matches Step 5 time
                0.50 24 7 4.1 ms
                0.80 17 14 2.9 ms
                0.95 (default) 14 17 3.0 ms

                Table 5. Threshold sweep over one set of overlaps: a test plot in Utrecht, 34 BAG buildings fetched, 31 candidates.

                B.5.2 3.2 Part 3: the whole recipe in one request

                With nested-processes, an input value may itself be an execution request: an object with a process key. The server runs that process first and puts its output in place of the input. The five steps then become one POST, and the intermediate values stay on the server.

                { "inputs": {
    "plan_dataid": "utrecht-demo",
    "overlaps": { "process": "plot-bag-overlap",
      "inputs": {
        "geometry": { "process": "plot-geometry-prepare",
                      "inputs": { "geometry": { "type": "Polygon", "coordinates": [ … ] } },
                      "outputs": { "geometry": {} } },
        "bag_buildings": { "process": "bag-panden-fetch",
                           "inputs": { "bbox": { "process": "plot-bbox",
                                                 "inputs": { "geometry": { … } } } },
                           "outputs": { "bag_buildings": {} } } },
      "outputs": { "overlaps": {} } } },
  "outputs": { "match_result": {} } }

                Listing 1. The five-step recipe as one nested request to bag-match-classify.

                Rules our server applies where the draft leaves room:

                Situation Our behaviour
                Nested process with several outputs You must request exactly one; otherwise 400 naming the outputs available
                Bare process ID ("plot-bbox") Resolved against this server, as a relative URI
                Absolute URI to another server 400: that is the remote-core-processes class, which we do not implement
                Failure inside a nested step Keeps its own status; a PDOK outage in step 3 is still 502
                Nesting deeper than 8 400; a cycle between processes shows up as this error, not a hang
                Top-level process key Ignored, as the draft requires

                Table 6. Nested execution rules.

                B.5.3 3.3 CWL as the recipe definition

                The console's Recipe tab reads a Common Workflow Language Workflow document in its JSON encoding. The diagram, the run order and the execution all come from that document, so editing steps changes what runs. CWL is the definition only: the steps are OGC processes, so run names a process ID (ogc:plot-bbox).

                "steps": {
  "prepare": { "run": "ogc:plot-geometry-prepare", "in": { "geometry": "geometry" },
               "out": ["geometry", "geometry_report"] },
  "bbox":    { "run": "ogc:plot-bbox", "in": { "geometry": "prepare/geometry" }, "out": ["bbox"] },
  "fetch":   { "run": "ogc:bag-panden-fetch", "in": { "bbox": "bbox/bbox" },
               "out": ["bag_buildings", "bag_query"] },
  "overlap": { "run": "ogc:plot-bag-overlap",
               "in": { "geometry": "prepare/geometry", "bag_buildings": "fetch/bag_buildings" },
               "out": ["overlaps", "skipped"] },
  "classify":{ "run": "ogc:bag-match-classify",
               "in": { "overlaps": "overlap/overlaps", "plan_dataid": "plan_dataid" },
               "out": ["match_result"] } }

                Listing 2. The steps of the recipe document.

                The console sorts the steps topologically from the in/out graph and reports a cycle instead of running it. It accepts in bindings as a source string, {"source": …} or {"default": …}. It rejects valueFrom instead of ignoring it, because dropping an expression would change what the workflow means.

                The same document runs two ways:

                Mode What happens Trade-off
                Step by step One request per step, sent from the browser You see every intermediate value; a shared step runs once
                One nested request The console compiles the graph into one Part 3 request One round trip, intermediates stay on the server; a shared step runs twice

                Table 7. Two execution modes from one CWL document.

                B.5.4 3.4 Measured results

                We ran the three routes against live PDOK from a laptop, with the server on localhost, 5 runs each, on 20 November 2026.

                Route Requests from client Median Min Max Same match_result as single call
                plot-bag-matching-pdok, one call 1 117 ms 81 ms 165 ms (reference)
                Five steps, one by one 5 128 ms 109 ms 192 ms yes
                Five steps, one nested request 1 152 ms 118 ms 240 ms yes

                Table 8. Recipe timings. The PDOK fetch (about 100 ms) dominates every route.

                On localhost a round trip costs almost nothing, so the nested request is not faster here. It does extra work, because it runs plot-geometry-prepare twice. The nested form wins when the client sits on a slow or distant network, where it saves 4 round trips and the transfer of the BAG features to the client and back.

                B.6 4. What we learned from building the console

                We built the console to test our own server from the position of a client vendor. It surfaced more interoperability questions than the server work did.

                B.6.1 4.1 A process description is almost enough to build a form

                The console turns each input's JSON Schema into a control: a text field, a number field with the schema's minimum and maximum, or a select for a boolean or an enum. Objects, arrays, oneOf and untyped schemas get a JSON editor with a Skeleton button that builds a starting value from the schema. That covers every input we have. Two gaps remain:

                • A GeoJSON polygon is "an object". The schema does not tell a generic client to offer a map. We supply example payloads in the console for structured inputs. GeoLabs reports the same gap in topic 2 and proposes a way to describe GeoJSON inputs.
                • null in a type array is a modifier, not a type. ["number", "null"] should render as an optional number field. The console picks the first non-null type to drive the control.

                B.6.2 4.2 The client needs two code paths for every response

                Ask for one output and you get the raw value. Ask for two and you get {"a": {"value": …}, "b": {"value": …}}. A recipe that passes outputs along has to know which shape it asked for and unwrap .value in the second case. plot-bag-matching-pdok declares two outputs, so a client that omits outputs gets the document form even when it only wants match_result. Our console always requests outputs explicitly and unwraps by the count it requested.

                B.6.3 4.3 The protocol is in the headers, and a browser hides them

                Location (where the job lives), Preference-Applied (whether the server honoured sync or async), Link (the results profile) and OGC-Output-Values-Count are all response headers. A browser script reads none of them on a cross-origin call unless the server lists them in Access-Control-Expose-Headers. A browser client against a server that forgets this still works for sync runs, and fails on async runs because it cannot find the job. Our CORS layer exposes all four and allows the Prefer and X-Api-Key request headers.

                B.6.4 4.4 Browser failures look like a server that is down

                Three different mistakes produced the same bare "network error":

                • A base URL typed without a scheme (api.example.nl). The browser resolved it as a path relative to the page. From a file:// page that path does not exist. The console now adds https:// (or http:// for localhost) and shows the URL it used.
                • Mixed content. An https page cannot call an http API. The browser blocks the request before it leaves.
                • A page opened from disk sends Origin: null. The server has to allow null, or *.

                The console names each cause in its error message. A client vendor should expect these on any Processes deployment behind a browser.

                B.6.5 4.5 Prefer is a request, not an instruction

                2.0 lets the server fall back to the mode a process supports. A client that sends Prefer: respond-async may get 200 with a result instead of 201 with a job. The console decides from the status code and from Preference-Applied, never from what it asked for.

                B.6.6 4.6 Nobody wires the steps for you

                Processes defines how to run one step. Part 3 nesting defines how to put one step's output into another's input, as a tree. Neither defines a recipe with shared steps, named intermediate results or a reusable definition. Our console fills that gap with CWL. Collaborall reached the same conclusion for recipes in topic 4 and chose the Open Workflow Specification. The testbed now has two recipe languages for the same job.

                B.7 5. Problems we ran into

                B.7.1 5.1 The validator passed a document it could not read

                Our first 2.0 /api was OpenAPI 3.1. The ogc-checker parses 3.0.x only. It skipped every rule and reported the document as valid. When we fed it a 3.0 copy of the same document it reported 21 errors: responses without schemas, the missing /jobs/{jobId}/results/{outputId}/0 path, and an outputs query parameter without style: form and explode: false. We moved /api to OpenAPI 3.0.3, fixed the 21 errors and do not claim the oas30 class, because we have not validated against it.

                Lesson: a green result from a validator proves nothing until you have seen it fail on your document.

                B.7.2 5.2 The 2.0 schemas have no stable home

                schemas.opengis.net does not publish the 2.0 schemas yet. Our OpenAPI document references them on the master branch of opengeospatial/ogcapi-processes. That branch moves, so a document that validates today may not validate next month. Two of the draft's own schema files carry broken $refs, which every 2.0 implementation inherits as invalid-ref errors. GeoLabs fixed several missing schemas upstream during the testbed.

                B.7.3 5.3 Go values are not JSON values

                Over HTTP, a step's output becomes JSON and the next step parses it back. Inside a nested request no HTTP happens. plot-bbox returns a Go [4]float64, and the next step's validator expects the []any that JSON parsing produces, so it rejected the bbox as "must be an array". We now round-trip every nested output through JSON before it becomes an input. A chained request then behaves like a chain of requests.

                B.7.4 5.4 Rounding and projection decide whether a split is exact

                • Rounding. The single call compares the unrounded overlap with the threshold, then rounds to 4 decimals. When step 4 rounded first, buildings near 0.95 crossed the threshold. Step 4 now returns the unrounded ratio and step 5 rounds after classifying.
                • Projection. Step 1 has to output GeoJSON, and our RD New transform runs one way only. Step 1 therefore repairs geometry in lon/lat, where the single call repairs it in RD New. For valid plots both agree exactly. For a self-intersecting plot the repaired parts can differ in the last decimals. Areas and overlaps use RD New in both routes.

                B.7.5 5.5 Our port and the notebook disagree on broken polygons

                The notebook repaired a self-intersecting "bowtie" with shapely's buffer(0), which kept one of the two lobes. Our repair splits the polygon at its self-intersections and keeps both lobes. We consider ours correct for a plot boundary, and plot-geometry-prepare reports every repair in geometry_report. A user comparing old notebook output with the process output will see this difference.

                B.7.6 5.6 PDOK limits shape the process

                • PDOK serves verblijfsobject, ligplaats and standplaats as points. Overlap is a share of area, so a point never matches. We query bag:pand only.
                • The WFS pages 1,000 features per request. We cap a run at 20,000 features and set truncated: true in bag_query when a plot hits the cap.
                • Querying the plot's bounding box loses no candidate, because every building that overlaps the plot also overlaps its bounding box.

                B.7.7 5.7 Smaller findings

                • 405 needs an Allow header. 2.0 asks for it and generic clients use it.
                • Problem types for bad input. The draft defines exception types for missing processes and jobs, not for invalid input. We return urn:ogc-api:exception:invalid-input, …:upstream-failure and …:process-failed for process failures. Schema validation failures still carry about:blank. A shared vocabulary would let clients act on the type.

                B.7.8 5.8 The pygeoapi plugin: schema, bad input and registration

                • The 1.0 schema rejected our process description. We validated the description against the official Processes 1.0 schema. That schema allows no bilingual title or description objects, and no type arrays such as ["integer", "null"]. We replaced the bilingual objects with plain strings and the type arrays with nullable: true. Our Go server's 2.0 descriptions use type arrays, for example ["object", "null"] (section 2.2).
                • Bad input crashed the plugin and left no trace. A review of the plugin found unhandled exceptions on a non-numeric threshold, an unreadable geometry and a self-intersecting polygon. The logs showed nothing. The plugin now validates its inputs and returns a process error for each case. It repairs invalid polygons itself and logs every rejection.
                • pygeoapi registers a plugin only once Python can import it. We added the package to the Python path.

                We then started a pygeoapi server and sent a request to the execution endpoint. The whole chain, from request to match result, worked.

                B.8 6. Where the effort went

                Relative effort, not measured hours.

                Area Effort Why
                Porting the calculation (notebook to Go) Medium Geometry without a geometry library: projection, area, self-intersection repair
                Processes 1.0 to 2.0 Medium Many small breaking changes, each simple
                OpenAPI document and validation High The 3.1 false pass, unpublished schemas and broken upstream $refs took the longest to attribute
                Splitting into exact steps Medium Rounding order and projection direction, found through an equality test
                Part 3 nested processes Low The JSON round trip was the only surprise
                Browser console Medium Most of the interoperability lessons in section 4 came from here
                pygeoapi plugin (Python, Processes 1.0) Medium Fitting the description to the 1.0 schema, input validation and error handling, plugin registration

                Table 9. Relative effort.

                Implementing the standard was the smaller part. Validating that we had implemented it, and making a generic client work against it, took more.

                B.9 7. Assessment of the standard

                B.9.1 7.1 What works well

                • Discovery is enough for a generic client. The console knows nothing about plots or BAG. It reads /processes, generates forms and runs every process on the server.
                • Sync and async share one endpoint. Prefer plus the status code lets one client handle both, and the fallback rule removes a class of 501 errors.
                • The job list filters cover monitoring. status, processID and duration filters gave us a job dashboard without extra endpoints.
                • Problem details. application/problem+json with a status and a detail gives every client the same error path.
                • Part 3 nesting is small. One rule, that an input may be an execution request, is enough to chain processes on the server.

                B.9.2 7.2 What we would change

                1. Make the response shape independent of the output count. Let a client ask for the document form explicitly, or always return it when outputs names one output. Today a recipe cannot pass outputs along without knowing how many it requested.
                2. Let Part 3 refer to a sibling output. A reference such as {"$output": "prepare/geometry"} would turn the tree into a graph. Without it, a shared step runs once per consumer.
                3. Publish the 2.0 schemas at a versioned, stable URL, and fix the broken $refs in value.yaml and valueNoObject.yaml before adoption.
                4. Standardise an exception type for invalid input and one for upstream failure.
                5. State in the standard which response headers a browser client needs, so servers expose them by default.

                B.10 8. Recommendations

                B.10.1 8.1 To Geonovum and the testbed programme

                • Have the ogc-checker fail, or warn loudly, when it receives an OpenAPI version it cannot parse.
                • Run the Topic 3 clients against every Topic 1 and 2 server and publish the matrix. Validation against the API definition says little about whether a client can use a server.
                • Choose one recipe language for the national digital twin. CWL and the Open Workflow Specification both came up in this testbed.

                B.10.2 8.2 To implementers

                • Build a client before you call your server done. Most of our interoperability findings came from the console, not from the validator.
                • Expose Location, Preference-Applied, Link and OGC-Output-Values-Count through CORS.
                • Write an equality test between a split recipe and its single-call version. Ours caught a rounding difference no review would have.
                • Report what the upstream returned (our bag_query) next to the result, so an empty answer is never ambiguous.

                B.11 9. Status, limitations and next steps

                Done

                • Processes 2.0 draft: core, ogc-process-description, json, job-list, callback, dismiss.
                • Part 3 nested-processes.
                • Plot to BAG matching as 2 single calls and 5 chainable steps, verified equal against live PDOK.
                • Browser console with CWL-driven recipes, step-by-step and nested.
                • Plot to BAG matching as a pygeoapi plugin on Processes 1.0, with input validation, polygon repair and logging.
                • Hardened container image and docker compose deployment; 199 automated test cases.

                Limitations

                • Jobs live in memory and disappear on restart. Production needs a persistent store.
                • Async jobs run as goroutines without a queue. That fits processes that finish in seconds.
                • We do not implement Part 2 (deploy, replace, undeploy) or the other seven Part 3 classes, among them remote-core-processes and cwl-workflows.
                • Inputs pass by value only; href inputs are rejected.

                Next steps

                • Deploy the Part 3 nested-processes build to the public endpoint.
                • Rerun ogc-checker and the OGC beta ETS on the final build and attach the output.
                • Replace the in-memory job store with a persistent one.
                • Follow Part 3 as it moves from version 0.0, and update the conformance URIs.

                B.12 10. Reproducing our results

                cd ogc-api
docker compose up --build          # API on http://localhost:8080

                Open ogc-api/web/index.html in a browser, set the base URL to http://localhost:8080 and press Connect. Or call the API directly:

                curl -X POST http://localhost:8080/processes/plot-bag-matching-pdok/execution \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" -H "Prefer: respond-sync" \
  -d '{"inputs": {"plot": {"plan_dataid": "utrecht-demo", "geometry": {"type": "Polygon",
        "coordinates": [[[5.1214,52.0907],[5.1230,52.0907],[5.1230,52.0918],[5.1214,52.0918],[5.1214,52.0907]]]}}},
       "outputs": {"match_result": {}}}'
                Variable Default Purpose
                PORT 8080 Listening port
                BASE_URL http://localhost:<PORT> Public base URL used in links
                JOB_MAX_AGE_HOURS 48 Evict jobs not accessed for this long
                CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS * Allowed browser origins; null allows file:// pages
                PDOK_BAG_WFS_URL https://service.pdok.nl/lv/bag/wfs/v2_0 BAG WFS, or a mirror or test double

                Table 10. Configuration.

                C. Topic 2: Geolabs

                From WPS to OGC API - Processes: the MapMint routing service on ZOO-Project-DRU

                Geonovum Testbed 2026, phase 2. Adoption topic #2: Complete and validate an existing OGC API Processes service

                Author: Gérald Fenoy, GeoLabs SARL (ORCID 0000-0002-9617-8641)

                License: CC BY 4.0

                C.1 Results at a glance

                Question Answer
                Is the service available through OGC API - Processes? Yes, at https://host1.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/ and https://host2.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/
                Does it pass the official OGC test suite (Part 1: Core 1.0)? Yes: 3 of 3 conformance classes; 53 tests passed, 0 failed, 1 skipped for host1
                Does it pass the Geonovum ogc-checker? Yes: 0 diagnostics of any kind, with the version 1.0.0 rules on host1 and with the version 2.0.0 rules on the second Server Instance host2
                Does it support sync and async execution and job management? Yes, on all three processes on the two server instances
                Does it work with version 2.0 of the standard (draft)? Yes, as far as the tools can tell: the host2 Server Instance passes ogc-checker 2.0.0 with 0 diagnostics; available in the ZOO-Project-DRU Helm chart since version 0.10.5. However, some endpoints are not yet implemented.
                Can other participants’ clients use the service? Partly, they can discover, describe, and run its processes. One client could also display the route on a map; another had issues detecting the input data types/formats, as discussed in C.7 Findings: describing GeoJSON inputs and outputs.
                What did the testbed deliver beyond the service? The routing service working as a process accessible from an OGC API - Processes - Part 1: Core Server Instance, three corrections merged into the official OGC schemas, Docker packaging merged into ogc-checker, two Server Instances for testing, and six recommendations (C.11 Observations and Recommendations).

                C.2 Summary

                GeoLabs enabled the MapMint platform's shortest-path service for Geonovum DTaaS Testbed 2026 Phase 2 through OGC API - Processes - Part 1: Core Standard. Previously available only via OGC Web Processing Service (WPS) 1.0.0, the service now runs on ZOO-Project-DRU, deployed on Kubernetes using the official Helm chart, with minor modifications to support the non-DRU ZOO-Project version.

                For the testbed, the service uses Dutch open data. The road network is the Nationaal Wegenbestand (NWB) from Rijkswaterstaat, comprising 1,652,111 road segments accessed via the PDOK OGC API - Features endpoint (NWB - Wegen (OGC API), 2026). Elevation data is sourced from AHN4 through the PDOK Web Coverage Service. Three processes, called in sequence, compute the shortest path and its elevation profile. The public NWB Route Explorer web application allows anyone to use these processes.

                The service is compliant, in the sense that it passes both the official OGC beta Executable Test Suite for Part 1: Core 1.0 and the Geonovum ogc-checker with no diagnostics reported.

                However, compliance alone does not guarantee interoperability. While clients from other participants could list and execute processes, only one client could display the route on a map. The investigation identified three issues:

                1. If the Server Instance passed every check/test, the output schema generated by the server could not be satisfied by any value;
                2. A client decides what to display on a map from a keyword (contentMediaType on an object) that the standard does not define for that purpose;
                3. The point inputs were literal plain strings; so no client was capable of offering a point picker, but was providing some field to use at least.

                A second Server Instance exposes a corrected process and passed all ogc-checker tests with version 2.0.0 of the OGC API - Processes - Part 1: Core. However, the input schema remained empty in the same client. This shows again that a process description can be valid yet unusable for clients.

                This report proposes a standard-compliant method for describing GeoJSON inputs and outputs. It provides six recommendations to the OGC API - Processes Standards Working Group (SWG), tool maintainers, client implementers, and Geonovum.

                Additionally, GeoLabs addressed gaps in the official OGC API - Processes schemas with three merged pull requests and contributed Docker packaging for ogc-checker.

                C.3 Objective and scope

                The Geonovum DTaaS Testbed 2026 Phase 2 aims to make the processes used by digital twins interoperable through OGC API - Processes, following the nLDT reference architecture. Adoption topic #2 asks for an existing implementation to be completed and validated.

                The objective was to migrate the MapMint routing service from WPS 1.0.0 to OGC API - Processes, demonstrate compliance, and test interoperability with other participants’ software.

                In scope Out of scope
                Process description (version 1.0, and version 2.0 where possible) Security layer (OAuth2 / API keys)
                Synchronous and asynchronous execution, job management, callbacks Other MapMint modules
                Validation with the OGC test suite and the Geonovum ogc-checker, with zero errors Recipes / CWL workflows (Topic #4)
                Public demonstration service with Dutch data (at least 6 months)
                Written report and demonstration video Geonovum_Testbed2026_GeoLabs_60s.mp4

                GeoLabs is a French company specialized in open-source geospatial software, founded in 2007 and based in Lattes. It is a core maintainer of the ZOO-Project, an OSGeo project and the OGC Reference Implementation of the OGC API - Processes - Part 1: Core Standard 1.0. GeoLabs also develops MapMint, a service-oriented GIS platform built on top of the ZOO-Project. The three most recent MapMint sales were to SNE (Chad), CEET (Togo) and NIGELEC (Niger).

                C.4 Implementation

                C.4.1 The routing service

                The MapMint routing service computes shortest paths on a road network stored in PostGIS and prepared with pgRouting, an extension of PostgreSQL/PostGIS providing geospatial routing functionality. The service has been available in MapMint since 2012, through WPS only. It was also the service used in the ZOO-Project workshops until these moved to OGC API - Processes. It is described in Choosumrong, Humhong, Raghavan & Fenoy (2019), Development of optimal routing service for emergency scenarios using pgRouting and FOSS4G, Korean Spatial Information Society.

                C.4.2 Architecture

                The deployment follows the three layers of the nLDT reference architecture: data, processes, and visualization. Figure 1 illustrates this architecture.

                The Server Instance runs on a Kubernetes cluster and was deployed using the official ZOO-Project-DRU Helm chart. The deployment files, the data-loading scripts, and the demonstration application are available in the GeoLabs/tb-geonovum-pgrouting repository.

                Component URL
                OGC API - Processes landing page https://host1.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/
                Built-in web interface (ZOO-Project) https://host1.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/index.html
                OpenAPI document https://host1.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/api
                NWB Route Explorer (demonstration application) https://host1.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/routing-ui/
                Second Server Instance (version 2.0 paths, routing1 process) https://host2.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/

                Both Server Instances are deployed without authentication, as the call for tenders required open access. The ZOO-Project nuxt-client, a web client application for Part 1 and Part 2 of the standard, can also connect to the Server Instance.

                C.4.3 Data

                • Road network: the wegvakken collection of the Nationaal Wegenbestand, read from the PDOK OGC API - Features endpoint using the GDAL ogr2ogr tool and loaded into PostGIS. It contains 1,652,111 road segments and is licensed CC0 1.0. The process consequently uses authoritative data through a standard API, with no file exchange.
                • Elevation: the AHN4 terrain model (dtm_05m, 0.5 m resolution), read on demand from the PDOK WCS 2.0.1 service for the area around each route.

                C.4.4 Processes

                Process Description
                routing Computes the shortest path between two points from the NWB network layer available in the PostGIS database, and returns a GeoJSON FeatureCollection, one feature per road segment.
                StageAHN Reprojects a route from EPSG:4326 to EPSG:28992 (RD New) and downloads the matching AHN4 terrain raster. This process stages the elevation data using a trivial WCS request over the full road extent. Obviously, extracting a smaller extent along the line would have made this step more efficient than it is currently.
                GdalExtractProfile Reads the elevation values (relying on the staged data previously by StageAHN) along the route from that raster.
                routing1 (on host2) Variant of routing that takes its two points as GeoJSON features (C.7 Findings: describing GeoJSON inputs and outputs).

                All processes support synchronous execution, asynchronous execution with job monitoring, and job dismissal.

                Below is an example of a request to execute the routing process.

                POST /ogc-api/processes/routing/execution HTTP/1.1
Host: host1.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr
Content-Type: application/json

{
  "inputs": {
    "startPoint": "5.364654064178468,52.160783637972834",
    "endPoint": "5.380897521972656,52.1533195002279",
    "corridorMeters": 5000
  },
  "outputs": { "Result": { "transmissionMode": "value" } },
  "response": "raw"
}

                The startPoint and endPoint inputs are lon,lat strings. The corridorMeters input defines the buffer around the two points for selecting candidate roads (default: 5000 m). The server returns the route as GeoJSON. If the Prefer: respond-async header is used, the server returns a job, which the client follows until the result is ready. The [article version] provides the full job status document and result.

                C.4.5 Demonstration application

                The NWB Route Explorer is a lightweight browser client developed for the testbed based on years of experience and available source code from the MapMint Open-Source software. Users select two points, and the application invokes the three processes in sequence, displaying each result as soon as it is available.

                In the example in Figure 2 and Figure 3, the route in Amersfoort is 1.47 km long and consists of 24 NWB segments. The elevation profile ranges from 0.70 to 6.88 m NAP, with the lowest points in the Brouwerstunnel, where the road passes under the railway near Amersfoort Centraal. The link between the profile and the map uses a technique from the 2010 ZOO-Project demonstration.

                C.5 Conformance

                Two independent tools were used. The OGC Executable Test Suite (ETS) invokes the running Server Instance and checks its responses. The Geonovum ogc-checker validates the OpenAPI description of the Server Instance. They test different aspects, and both are needed.

                C.5.1 OGC test suite, Part 1: Core 1.0

                The official OGC beta ETS (ogcapi-processes-1.0, version 1.2) was run against host1. The full report is available in s0051.zip.

                As expected from an official reference implementation, the three conformance classes pass, and the report confirms that the implementation meets the certification requirements. The skipped test requires an array input, which none of the deployed processes has.

                C.5.2 ogc-checker

                On the host1 Server Instance, with all the demonstration processes deployed, the checker (version 1.0.0 rules) reports:

                Applied rulesets: core, job-list, json, ogc-process-description (ogcapi-processes-1/1.0)
Diagnostics: 0 (errors 0, warnings 0, info 0, hints 0)

                In this report, "zero errors" means zero diagnostics of any severity. C.13 Appendix A. Reproducing the ogc-checker run gives the commands to reproduce this run.

                C.5.3 Version 2.0 (draft) and the second Server Instance

                Version 2.0 of the standard is still a draft under public review when we write this report. Testing against this upcoming version of the Standard was instructive and revealed small gaps in the official schemas, which required corrections that were handled and submitted upstream (C.9 Contributions to upstream projects). Since version 0.10.5 of the official ZOO-Project-DRU Helm chart, a parameter (zoo.processesv2) makes the Server Instance publish a version 2.0 OpenAPI description.

                A second Server Instance, host2, was deployed on 16 November 2026 to confirm this on a live server. With the version 2.0.0 rules, ogc-checker reports no diagnostics of any kind:

                Ruleset: ogc-api-processes@2.0.0
Applied rulesets: http://www.opengis.net/spec/ogcapi-processes-1/2.0/conf/core,
  http://www.opengis.net/spec/ogcapi-processes-1/2.0/conf/job-list,
  http://www.opengis.net/spec/ogcapi-processes-1/2.0/conf/json,
  http://www.opengis.net/spec/ogcapi-processes-1/2.0/conf/ogc-process-description
Diagnostics: 0 (errors 0, warnings 0, info 0, hints 0)
No diagnostics.

                This Server Instance declares the Core conformance classes of both version 1.0 and version 2.0, and its OpenAPI description includes the version 2.0 paths.

                C.5.4 Points of attention

                The OGC API - Processes - Part 2: Deploy, Replace, Undeploy draft specification is not supported by these Server Instances. Their OpenAPI descriptions list the DRU endpoints and conformance classes only to permit ogc-checker to validate the DRU schemas. The conformance declaration should consequently not be read as a capability to deploy processes.

                The ZOO-Project-DRU Helm chart, versions lower than 0.10.5, failed validation using the ogc-checker because it referenced schemas that had been relocated, renamed, or were unavailable. One schema, NotSupported.yaml, still has no official location identified. Further details are available in the discussion #4.

                C.6 Interoperability with other participants

                GeoLabs took part in the first plugfest (28 September 2026) remotely and in the second one (27 October 2026) in person.

                The client applications developed by other participants could list the processes, read their descriptions, and execute them. Displaying the result on a map was another matter.

                Client Executes routing Displays the route on a map
                Breinstein OGC API - Processes client (Topic #3) Yes Yes, from the first plugfest
                Imagem PLANSPACE Simulator (Topic #3), routing on host1 Yes No
                Imagem PLANSPACE Simulator (Topic #3), routing1 on host2 No: the form has no input field No

                C.6.1 Breinstein client

                The Breinstein client was able to both invoke routing and draw the route on its own map, with no change required on either side.

                The client creates a form from the process description provided by the Server Instance. Depending on the schema found, it displays the optional search corridor and its default value, and lets users choose how they receive the result. No relay is involved: requests can go directly from the browser to the Server Instance, as it is configured to send the CORS headers browsers require.

                C.6.2 PLANSPACE Simulator

                During the second plugfest, the routing process was executed by typing the inputs in the form, but no result was displayed. The PLANSPACE team indicated that the output description needed at least the application/geo+json content type (contentMediaType). As this did not match our understanding of the standard, it was investigated further (C.7 Findings: describing GeoJSON inputs and outputs).

                Later, a new test was run with routing1 on host2, whose inputs and output are described with references to GeoJSON schemas. The result was worse: the form has no input field at all, as illustrated in Figure 5. On the same Server Instance, the form for StageAHN does show its inputs, but reduces the route geometry to a selector with "option 1", as illustrated in Figure 6.

                C.7 Findings: describing GeoJSON inputs and outputs

                Note that every description discussed in this section passes the OGC test suite and ogc-checker.

                C.7.1 The output schema could not be satisfied

                On host1, the processDescription generated by the ZOO-Kernel for the route output of routing has a schema that is defined as follows:

                "schema": {
  "oneOf": [
    { "type": "object" },
    { "type": "object" }
  ]
}

                oneOf means "exactly one of these". A GeoJSON object matches both branches, so it is rejected, as is any other value. A JSON Schema validator confirmed that even the route returned by the service is not valid against this schema.

                For this specific issue, the cause is internal to the ZOO-Kernel. As the process metadata declares two output formats, application/geo+json and application/json, the ZOO-Kernel then creates one branch per format, but as both are "type": "object", they all become "type": "object".

                This was not noticed before, because client applications do not validate results against the schema. Nevertheless, the schema also tells a client nothing about the data. In the PLANSPACE Simulator, the same pattern used on an input produces the "option 1" selector shown in Figure 6.

                C.7.2 A client convention that is not in the standard

                Based on our understanding of the PLANSPACE Simulator and our discussion with the IMAGEM team present during the second plugfest, it seems the client application searches for a single top-level element: contentMediaType in the schema. If it is not found, the client disregards the inputs/outputs definition and any inputs/outputs following it.

                In the process description What the client displays
                output with contentMediaType = application/geo+json a GeoJSON layer on the map
                input with contentMediaType = application/geo+json drawing on the map
                contentMediaType = image/tiff; application=geotiff a GeoTIFF layer on the map

                The client application does not look at type or format, and does not follow $ref references either. To know what the user may draw, it requires the GeoJSON type names to be directly available within the schema.

                This differs from our understanding of the standard, where process descriptions use JSON Schema, in which contentMediaType applies to strings only and has no effect on objects. The examples of the standard use contentMediaType only on strings. For GeoJSON carried as a JSON object, they use the format key, with the geojson-feature-collection, geojson-feature and geojson-geometry values.

                The schema that the client expects would pass validation, because the meta-schema of the standard accepts both keywords. Consequently, servers and clients in the testbed agreed on a convention that works between them, but not the one defined by the standard.

                The test with routing1 confirms it. Its description is valid and passes ogc-checker:

                "schema": {
  "oneOf": [
    {
      "type": "object",
      "$ref": "https://schemas.opengis.net/ogcapi/features/part1/1.0/openapi/schemas/featureGeoJSON.yaml"
    }
  ]
}

                The client shows no input for this process, not even the plain number input that it displays correctly for StageAHN. The whole form fails. The likely cause is the reference to an external schema, which the form generator of the client does not resolve. This has not been verified inside the client.

                C.7.3 The point inputs were plain strings

                The startPoint and endPoint inputs were strings, and nothing in the description indicated that they had to follow the lon,lat format. A client application can only offer a point picker on a map if the input is described as GeoJSON.

                C.8 Proposal

                As a solution, GeoLabs would propose to describe GeoJSON values the way the standard intends, and to update the ZOO-Kernel accordingly, to provide a schema containing:

                • an object branch with the format key and a $ref to the official GeoJSON schema;
                • a string branch with contentMediaType, where JSON Schema gives it a meaning.

                The schema definition below for the route output is intended to make the process work with IMAGEM’s PLANSPACE simulator. Providing such a schema definition from a process description generated by the ZOO-Project is out of scope from the testbed perspective.

                "schema": {
  "oneOf": [
    {
      "allOf": [
        { "format": "geojson-feature-collection" },
        { "$ref": "https://geojson.org/schema/FeatureCollection.json" }
      ]
    },
    { "type": "string", "contentMediaType": "application/geo+json" }
  ]
}

                Below is the proposed schema for the two point inputs, which would take a GeoJSON point instead of a lon,lat string.

                "schema": {
  "oneOf": [
    {
      "allOf": [
        { "format": "geojson-geometry" },
        { "$ref": "https://geojson.org/schema/Point.json" }
      ]
    },
    { "type": "string", "contentMediaType": "application/geo+json" }
  ]
}

                Both schemas were checked using a JSON Schema validator and the official GeoJSON schemas:

                Value Current output schema Proposed output schema Proposed input schema
                The route, as an object Rejected Valid —
                The route, as a string Rejected Valid —
                A GeoJSON Point, as an object Rejected Rejected Valid
                A GeoJSON Point, as a string — — Valid
                A GeoJSON LineString, as an object — — Rejected

                In WPS, distinguishing between input and output formats/types was based on XSD schemas that we could associate with mimeType. Now, with OGC API - Processes - Part 1: Core, the metadata for a process must define the JSON schema for each input and output. This can be explained by the media type, which cannot, on its own, distinguish a feature collection from a feature or a geometry. The ZOO-Kernel must try to avoid producing identical branches, add the format value, and keep contentMediaType for strings. One can note that the following format choice supported by the input and output of a process is correctly exposed for binary data, where the binary data is transferred as a base64-encoded string and embedded in the extended-schema associated with the schema for a given input/output. The routing1 process deployed on host2 is a first step: it references GeoJSON schemas and has a single branch, but does not yet include the format key or the string branch.

                Changes for client applications. Changing the inputs to GeoJSON breaks the current callers, including the NWB Route Explorer, which will have to send points such as {"type": "Point", "coordinates": [5.364654, 52.160784]}. The route itself does not change.

                What is not proposed. The PLANSPACE Simulator would still not work with these schemas. A server could add "contentMediaType": "application/geo+json" at the top of the schema only for this client. As this keyword has no meaning on an object in JSON Schema, it is left out. It makes more sense for the client to recognise format and follow $ref, so that any description following the standard works.

                C.9 Contributions to upstream projects

                Contribution Where Status
                #625 "Add outputs schema" (Part 1: Core) opengeospatial/ogcapi-processes Merged, 8 September 2026
                #626 "Add the missing rPackage response" (Part 2: DRU) opengeospatial/ogcapi-processes Merged, 8 September 2026
                #627 "Add application/cwl media-type" (Part 2: DRU) opengeospatial/ogcapi-processes Merged, 8 September 2026
                #857 Dockerfile and documentation Geonovum/ogc-checker Merged
                Version 2.0 OpenAPI generation and corrected schema references ZOO-Project-DRU Helm chart Released in 0.10.5, 1 October 2026

                C.10 Limitations

                The OGC test suite runs its execution tests on an echo process and examines three processes only. It demonstrates that the Server Instance implements the standard, not that the routing process itself was tested. The beta instance of the test suite was used, not a certification run.

                For version 2.0, the evidence is the ogc-checker validation of the host2 OpenAPI description. The OGC test suite targets version 1.0 and was not run against host2.

                The interoperability observations were made by a single participant during two plugfests, using the client applications that were accessible.

                The proposed schemas were checked using a validator. They are not yet implemented in the ZOO-Kernel.

                The quality and the performance of the routing itself were not measured.

                C.11 Observations and Recommendations

                As illustrated in this activity, a service that had only ever been offered through WPS can be made available through OGC API - Processes, on Dutch open data, and pass both the OGC test suite and the Geonovum ogc-checker. This fulfills the topic's primary objective.

                More importantly, the findings show what compliance checks missed. One description passed every test despite containing a schema that no value could satisfy. Another, corrected description also passed every test, yet produced an empty form in a client. Current tools verify that descriptions are well formed, but not that clients can use them. Bridging this gap will require clearer guidance in the standard, more rigorous validation tools, and clients that interpret the standard’s defined keywords.

                To Recommendation
                R1 OGC API - Processes SWG Version 2.0 should state explicitly how GeoJSON inputs and outputs must be described, and that contentMediaType applies to strings only.
                R2 OGC API - Processes SWG Every schema reference in the specification and its examples should resolve, and the NotSupported response should be given an official location.
                R3 Compliance-tool maintainers The tools should warn about identical oneOf branches, and about contentMediaType on an object used as the only indication that a value is GeoJSON.
                R4 Client implementers Client applications should recognize the format key based on a predefined and referenceable vocabulary and/or follow $ref references in process descriptions.
                R5 Geonovum An official, versioned ogc-checker Docker image should be published, so that anyone can run the same check with a single command and state which version of the rules was used.
                R6 Geonovum and OGC A profile or Building Block defining richer descriptions of geospatial inputs and outputs should be considered.

                C.12 Next steps

                • Update the ZOO-Kernel and the .zcfg format as proposed in C.8 Proposal, and describe the routing inputs as GeoJSON points.
                • Test the result with the testbed client applications, and share C.7 Findings: describing GeoJSON inputs and outputs with the PLANSPACE team and with the OGC API - Processes SWG.
                • Point the Helm chart to the official outputs.yaml and rPackage.yaml schemas now that they are merged upstream.
                • Follow up on the NotSupported.yaml reference with the SWG.
                • Keep the demonstration service online for at least six months after the testbed.

                C.13 Appendix A. Reproducing the ogc-checker run

                Until an official image is available (recommendation R5), the image has to be built locally:

                git clone https://github.com/Geonovum/ogc-checker.git
cd ogc-checker
docker build . -t ogc-checker:latest
curl -o test.json https://host1.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/api
docker run --rm -v "$PWD/test.json:/data/test.json:ro" \
    ogc-checker:latest validate --standard ogc-api-processes --input /data/test.json

                To check the version 2.0 description of host2, the same image is used with the --version 2.0.0 option:

                curl -o test.json https://host2.tb.geonovum.geolabs.fr/ogc-api/api
docker run --rm -v "$PWD/test.json:/data/openapi.json:ro" \
    ogc-checker:latest validate --standard ogc-api-processes --version 2.0.0 --input /data/openapi.json
