                D. Topic 3: Breinstein

                A browser client for OGC API – Processes

                Geonovum nLDT Testbed 2026, Phase 2
                Topic 3: OGC API Processes client
                Author: Breinstein

                D.1 Abstract

                OGC API – Processes lets a client discover, run and monitor computational processes over a Web API. In the Dutch national digital twin architecture (nLDT), a visualisation front end calls computational modules through this standard. Existing conformance testing checks servers from outside the browser, so it cannot show whether a web page can use them. Of the existing open-source client libraries, most do not cover Processes. The one that does reduces each response to its JSON body, so it loses the Location header that asynchronous execution depends on.

                We built a browser client in two layers:

                • a runtime-neutral protocol core, published on npm;
                • a web interface on top of it, which generates input forms and shows results on a map, so a user can run a process without writing any code.

                The client speaks the 2.0 draft of the standard where a service declares it, and falls back to 1.0 otherwise. With it, we tested six deployments: two implementations run by the team throughout the testbed (pygeoapi and ZOO-Project), and three services run by testbed parties. We recorded every point at which a service's behaviour differed from the standard, from its own declarations, or from what a browser allows: 72 filed findings.

                The main results:

                • CORS decides whether a web page can use a service at all, not OGC conformance. Three of the six deployments can be reached directly from a web page. Only one of those three lets a page read the Location header that names a new asynchronous job.
                • Declarations and behaviour diverge in both directions. Capabilities that work are not declared, and capabilities that are declared or listed do not work.
                • Neither team-run implementation validates execute inputs, and in 16 findings a server answers with a success status while doing something other than what was asked.
                • The same request returns differently shaped answers from different implementations.

                We considered a server-side relay to work around these limits, and built one. We decided not to deploy it publicly, for security reasons. Instead we recommend that services configure CORS correctly, and that any access control they need uses a documented scheme that a browser can send. We also propose profile guidance, and identify four gaps in the standard itself.

                D.2 1. Introduction

                The nLDT architecture separates visualisation from computation. A viewer finds a computational module through a catalogue and runs it through OGC API – Processes [13, 14]. For this separation to work, any conformant viewer must be able to drive any conformant computational module. In practice the viewer is often a web application, so the client runs inside a browser, under the browser's security rules.

                Several general-purpose client libraries for OGC API – Processes exist (section 2.4), but no mature client offers the standard a visual, easy-to-use interface. QGIS, for example, supports OGC API – Features as a client, but its documentation lists no support for OGC API – Processes [18]. To try a process, a user must therefore turn to developer tools such as Swagger UI, Postman or cURL. A client in the web browser lowers that barrier: it requires no installation, runs on any platform, and can show results directly on a map. Within the testbed, where adoption topics 1 and 2 provide the server side of the standard, such a client is the counterpart that exercises those services. It is deliberately independent of any single use case, so that every participant's processes become a test of interoperability.

                Topic 3 of the 2026 testbed asks for such a client. This report addresses three questions:

                • RQ1. Can a browser client use an OGC API – Processes service directly, without a server-side intermediary?
                • RQ2. Where do implementations deviate from the standard, or from their own declarations, in ways that affect an interoperable client?
                • RQ3. What should service operators and an nLDT profile do to close those gaps?

                The report treats the services tested alike. Results are grouped by theme rather than by service (section 5). Every filed finding is listed in Annex A.

                D.3 2. Background

                D.3.1 2.1 The standard

                OGC API – Processes – Part 1: Core 1.0.0 (OGC 18-062r2) was approved in August 2021 and published in December 2021 [1]. It builds on concepts from the Web Processing Service (WPS) 2.0 standard, but replaces its XML operations with a resource-oriented API using JSON and OpenAPI [1].

                The standard defines:

                • a process list (/processes) and a process description per process;
                • synchronous execution, where the result comes back in the response;
                • asynchronous execution, where the server creates a job and returns its address in the Location header;
                • job status, results and dismissal (/jobs/{jobID}, /results, DELETE);
                • optional callbacks to a subscriber URL.

                A version 2 of Part 1 was put out for public comment in August 2026 [2]. The client follows the draft of that version (section 3.2). Every clause cited in this report refers to 1.0.0.

                18-062r2 does not define an authentication scheme. It recommends access control on the job list and on dismissal (Security Considerations), defines 401 and 403, and requires an access-controlled service that implements the OpenAPI 3.0 class to document its security scheme in its OpenAPI definition (Requirement 63) [1]. How a client authenticates is left to each deployment.

                D.3.2 2.2 Conformance testing

                The OGC validator runs an executable test suite (ETS) for Part 1 in TEAM Engine. The suite was developed in OGC Testbed-17 [4]. It covers the Core, OGC Process Description, JSON, HTML, OpenAPI 3.0 and Job list conformance classes [3]. The tester names one echo process, which is used for the execute tests [3].

                Two properties of this approach matter here:

                • The test harness is not a browser. Cross-origin rules (CORS) are enforced only by browsers. A server can pass every test and still be unreachable from a web page.
                • The Dismiss and Callback classes are not in the suite's list [3]. They are also the classes that matter most to a client that monitors jobs.

                D.3.3 2.3 Implementations

                pygeoapi is an open-source Python server for the OGC API family. Its documentation lists OGC API – Processes as "Compliant" in its standards support matrix [5]. Processes are added as Python plugins. Asynchronous execution is requested with Prefer: respond-async and needs a job manager [6].

                ZOO-Project is an OSGeo project: a C-based platform that implements WPS 1.0.0 and 2.0.0 as well as OGC API – Processes [16]. It ships several hundred ready-made processes built on libraries such as GDAL, SAGA GIS and the Orfeo Toolbox.

                We ran both ourselves, pinned, for the whole testbed:

                • pygeoapi 0.21.0 is the server in the blocking continuous-integration lane. It is widely deployed, it implements callbacks, and its CORS can be switched on and off by configuration.
                • ZOO-Project is the second implementation. It helps tell a server's deviation from an ambiguous clause in the specification, and its catalogue of more than 700 processes gives the form generator real variety.

                We did not establish which releases were certified, so our observations are not a statement about certification.

                D.3.4 2.4 Existing clients

                • ogcapi-js is a TypeScript library with a package for OGC API – Processes [7]. In our review, its shared request helper reduces a successful response to its JSON body, so headers such as Location and Retry-After never reach the caller.
                • ogc-client is a TypeScript library for WMS, WFS, WMTS and OGC API Records and Features [8]. It reports when a service cannot be used because of CORS [8], but does not cover Processes.
                • OWSLib is a Python client library for OGC services, including the OGC API family [9]. It runs outside the browser.

                We found no browser client for OGC API – Processes that keeps the response headers asynchronous execution depends on, or that records how each server had to be reached.

                D.3.5 2.5 Browser constraints

                Under the WHATWG Fetch standard [10], a page may read a response from another origin only when the server allows it with CORS headers. Two further rules apply:

                • Requests with a JSON body, a Prefer header, an Authorization header or the DELETE method need a preflight: an OPTIONS request that the server must also allow.
                • Script can read only a small set of response headers. Location is not one of them, so the server must list it in Access-Control-Expose-Headers.

                18-062r2 anticipates both problems. Section 7.6 of 18-062r2 recommends supporting cross-origin requests for browser use (Recommendation 4) and exposing Location (Recommendation 5) [1]. Both are recommendations, not requirements.

                D.3.6 2.6 Contribution

                Existing work What we take from it What we add
                OGC 18-062r2 [1] and the 2.0 draft The requirements and schemas each observation is checked against Observations of behaviour the requirements leave open, and of recommendations that are not followed
                OGC ETS for Part 1 [3, 4] Testing against a running server Testing from a real browser, and of the Dismiss and Callback classes
                pygeoapi and ZOO-Project [5, 6, 16] Two pinned open-source implementations, one in two CORS configurations A record of where each departs from the standard and from its own declarations
                ogcapi-js [7] An inventory of operations and types, and test fixtures, corrected where needed A response envelope that keeps status, headers and body as received
                ogc-client [8] Link-following, error handling, and reporting CORS failures to the user The same discipline applied to Processes, including jobs and callbacks
                OGC code sprints [12] Reporting interoperability results back to standards editors Findings graded on a fixed vocabulary, with the evidence published

                D.4 3. The client

                In the absence of a reusable client, every viewer that is to call a computational module must implement the protocol itself. This includes discovery, conformance negotiation, paging, execution, polling and the retrieval of results. It also includes tolerances for each server's deviations from the standard (section 5), and an interface for the inputs and outputs of every process. With N viewers and M modules, this amounts to N × M separate integrations. A shared standard reduces them to N + M components, each of which implements the standard once, so that any viewer can be paired with any module. This is what the nLDT architecture asks for when it requires models and visualisation tools to be reusable and exchangeable, and open standards to prevent vendor lock-in [14]. The implementation presented here addresses it in two layers, each of which can be used without the other: a protocol core (section 3.1), and a web interface built on it (section 3.3).

                Figure 1 shows these parts and how they depend on each other. A third part, the relay, is optional and is discussed in section 6. The build enforces the boundaries between the parts. The core may import no framework, no map library and neither application, and within the web interface only the map module may use MapLibre and Terra Draw.

                Figure 1. The architecture of the client. The dashed outline marks the MIT-licensed repository, which holds three parts. The web interface (React, TypeScript, MapLibre) generates forms, follows jobs and shows results on a map; it uses the protocol core only through the core's public API. The protocol core (@breinstein/oap-client on npm) implements the protocol and depends on no framework or map library. It sends its HTTP requests directly from the browser to the services. The relay (dashed box) is optional and is not deployed beside the public interface: it would receive callbacks and reach services without CORS on the page's behalf (section 6). "Another viewer" stands for any web or Node application that installs the core from npm and embeds it, without our web interface. It shows the intended reuse; no such integration is known to exist yet. Solid arrows are the paths in use; dashed arrows are paths through the relay.

                D.4.1 3.1 Protocol core

                The core is published on npm as @breinstein/oap-client [15]. Release 0.6.0 appeared on 18 November 2026, after seven earlier releases from 26 August. The core is written in TypeScript and published as JavaScript with type declarations, so it can be used from any JavaScript application, with or without TypeScript.

                It handles:

                • discovery and conformance;
                • process lists and descriptions;
                • synchronous and asynchronous execution;
                • polling, dismissal and results.

                Four properties make it a basis rather than an application:

                • It depends on no framework and no map library. Lint rules and dependency checks enforce this in the build.
                • It is runtime-neutral. It uses no browser-only or Node-only API, and consumer smoke tests run the packed package in both kinds of environment.
                • fetch is passed in by the caller. An embedding application can add its own headers, for example an API key, without changing the core. The core README shows the pattern.
                • It keeps evidence. Every response is kept as it arrived (status, headers, media type, body) and classified by what arrived. Every deviation it tolerates is reported to the caller as an observation, not smoothed over.

                The core is built to work with services that depart from the standard, without hiding that they do:

                • It follows links rather than building paths. Every address comes from a link the service advertises, resolved against the address the document was actually served from. Both the short and the long form of a link relation are accepted, because pygeoapi uses both on one landing page (0005).
                • It asks for JSON explicitly. Every request sends Accept: application/json, and an HTML answer counts as a failure. A service that ignores the header is asked once more with f=json, and the route that worked is recorded (0007, 0036).
                • It treats a capability as advertised, assumed or neither. Dismissal and callbacks have conformance classes of their own; synchronous and asynchronous execution do not, so the core can only assume them. A missing class never stops a request: pygeoapi supports dismissal and the job list without declaring either (0006).

                Section 8.4 turns these choices into practices for other clients.

                D.4.2 3.2 Version negotiation

                The client speaks version 2 where a service supports it and falls back to 1.0 otherwise. "Version 2" means the draft OGC 18-062r3, pinned at commit 756b659 of the standard's repository (20 October 2026), because version 2 is not yet a published standard. All differences sit in one dated module, so a change in the draft changes one file.

                The service declares The client speaks
                the 2.0 Core class, with or without 1.0 Core the 2.0 draft
                only the 1.0 Core class 1.0
                neither, or no readable conformance 1.0, the published standard

                Fallback is a choice made before sending, never a retry. Executing a process is not idempotent, so a request sent as 2.0 and resent as 1.0 could start two jobs.

                Release. Version 2 support is in the npm release 0.6.0 and in the web interface. We checked the published package against a simulated service:

                The simulated service declares Version chosen Request for both outputs as a document Request for one output, raw Request for two outputs, raw
                2.0 and 1.0 Core 2.0 draft, from the declaration no response member; the server decides from the outputs requested the one output listed, no response refused before sending
                1.0 Core only 1.0, from the declaration response: "document" response: "raw" response: "raw"
                nothing (conformance 404) 1.0, by default as 1.0 as 1.0 as 1.0

                Asynchronous execution sends Prefer: respond-async in both versions. A caller can pin a version; on a 2.0 service pinned to 1.0, the choice is recorded as configured.

                Two qualifications apply:

                • Evidence. Weaver is the only server found that declares the 2.0 Core class. The core has completed synchronous and asynchronous runs against it from Node. No run has yet succeeded from the browser, because of Weaver's own limits: no CORS headers, a single runnable process, and a refusal of the draft's encoding of that process's inputs.
                • Elsewhere. Of the services in this report, Roofer Online declares the 2.0 Core class, but cannot be reached from a browser. pygeoapi (including 0.24.0) and ZOO-Project declare 1.0 only.

                D.4.3 3.3 Web interface

                The web interface is a React application written in TypeScript, with MapLibre GL JS for the map. It is built on the core's public API: 31 of its source files import the package. The package exports only its public entry point, so the interface cannot reach into the core's internals. For a user it provides:

                • Generated forms. Inputs are generated from each process description: text, numbers, choices, dates and files. Bounding boxes and geometries are drawn on the map with Terra Draw, a drawing library that works through adapters with MapLibre, OpenLayers, Leaflet and other map libraries [19]. A viewer built on another map library can therefore offer the same drawing tools. A schema the generator cannot render falls back to raw JSON input (section 5.11).
                • Job handling. Synchronous and asynchronous runs, a job list, polling and dismissal.
                • Results on the map. GeoJSON, GML and KML are drawn on a map. Results in RD New (EPSG:28992) are reprojected. Images are shown inline, tables as tables, JSON and text as they arrived, and anything else is offered as a download.
                • Example processes. The start screen offers ready-made examples that open a process with one click, so a user can try the client without knowing a service address. The hosted interface offers five, from a demonstration service set up for the testbed: how green an area is, the feels-like temperature on a hot day, where the shade is, roof space for solar panels, and the age of the buildings. They are written for users without a technical background, such as policy makers. The demonstration service runs on the same server as the hosted interface; its code is not public.

                A user of the hosted interface can connect to a service, fill in a generated form and see the result on a map without writing any code. A developer who embeds the core in their own viewer still writes their own forms and rendering: those parts belong to the web application, which is not published as a package. They are available as MIT source in the repository.

                D.4.4 3.4 Out of scope

                • Authentication. The web interface has no setting for an API key or a token. 18-062r2 defines no authentication scheme (section 2.1), so nothing in the standard tells a client what to send. Through the core, a developer can add one with their own fetch.
                • Callbacks. The hosted interface does not use callbacks; it polls (section 6).
                • Servers without CORS. The hosted interface cannot reach them. Section 6 explains why.

                D.4.5 3.5 Walkthrough

                This section follows one user through the web interface, from connecting to a service to reading a result on the map. The screenshots were taken from client release 0.6.0, run locally against the reference pygeoapi (sections 2.3 and 4.1). The hosted interface at https://oap.breinstein.nl works the same way, but offers other example services on its start screen.

                1. Connect to a service. The start screen asks for one thing: the address of a service's landing page. The user can also pick a known service or start from an example process (Figure 2).

                Figure 2. The start screen.

                2. Choose a process. After connecting, the client lists the service's processes. For each one it shows what the user will need to provide, read from the process description: an area to draw, data to upload, or plain values (Figure 3).

                Figure 3. The processes a service offers, and what each needs.

                3. Fill in the generated form. Opening a process shows its description and a form generated from the JSON Schema of each input (Figure 4). An area can be typed as GeoJSON, loaded from a file, taken from the current map view, or drawn. A checkbox runs the process in the background.

                <img src="./media/using-03-form.png" alt="The form for "Buildings in an area", with the map beside it" width="900">

                Figure 4. A form generated from the process description.

                4. Draw the area. The drawing tools add an area or a box on the map. The form fills in the GeoJSON as the user draws, and shows the size, here "about 170 m across", so the user can see it is within the process's limit of 1 km (Figure 5). A place search moves the map to an address.

                Figure 5. An area drawn on the map, in Amersfoort.

                5. Read the result. When the process finishes, the result appears on the map in blue, beside the area the user sent, which is shown as a dashed outline (Figure 6). Clicking a building shows its properties. The panel names the format, offers a download, and repeats the inputs that were sent, so the user can check what the service was asked.

                Figure 6. The buildings in the area, from Kadaster's BAG via PDOK.

                6. Run a long process in the background. With "Run in the background" ticked, the service creates a job and the client follows it (Figure 7). The page shows the job's status and progress, and gives a link that opens the same job from any browser. It also offers to cancel the job, and says when the service does not declare that it supports cancelling (finding 0006).

                Figure 7. A job running in the background, here on pygeoapi 0.24.0.

                7. Find jobs later. The jobs panel lists every background job started in the browser, with its status, and keeps it after the page is reloaded (Figure 8). From there the user can open the results or run the process again.

                Figure 8. The jobs panel.

                When a service hides the job. Against pygeoapi 0.21.0, the client cannot learn which job it started, because the service does not expose Location to the page (finding 0039). The jobs panel then stays empty, and says why (Figure 9). This is the limit that sections 5.3 and 8.1 describe, as a user meets it.

                Figure 9. The same panel against a service that hides Location.

                D.5 4. Method

                D.5.1 4.1 Services

                Service Version Run by Role Findings
                pygeoapi, CORS on and off 0.21.0, pinned the team reference server, blocking in continuous integration 36
                ZOO-Project pinned fork of upstream; 703 processes the team second implementation, non-blocking 45
                ZOO-Project-DRU hosted testbed deployment third party testbed service 3
                Roofer Online staging third party testbed service 1
                Varidian registry as of 27 and 31 October 2026 third party testbed service none filed
                Weaver 6.16.0 plus master the team the only server found declaring 2.0 Core recorded in the core's README

                13 findings cover both pygeoapi and ZOO-Project, so the counts add up to more than the 72 filed findings.

                ZOO-Project-DRU is a build of ZOO-Project that also implements the draft Part 2 of the standard, Deploy, Replace, Undeploy, with which users add, update and remove processes through the API itself [17]. The client uses only Part 1, so this deployment was tested as an ordinary OGC API – Processes service.

                pygeoapi runs:

                • the stock hello-world process;
                • seventeen processes written for the testbed, each covering one case the stock process does not: binary output, several outputs, bounding-box and geometry inputs, inputs by reference, long-running jobs, single-mode execution and others.

                Browser behaviour was tested in Chromium with Playwright. Protocol behaviour was tested with contract tests and captured requests and responses.

                D.5.2 4.2 Grading

                A finding is recorded whenever a service's behaviour affects an interoperable client. Each finding states:

                • what was sent and what came back, with the captured payload;
                • the clause, requirement or recommendation it was checked against;
                • what the client does about it.
                Scale Values
                Outcome conformant; non-conformant (deviates from a citable clause); undeclared (works but is not advertised); declared-not-working (advertised but does not work); unsupported (absent, and legitimately so); blocked (could not be tested)
                Severity blocking (a required capability cannot be delivered); major (a client doing the obvious thing breaks); minor (works, at a cost); informational
                Confidence high (reproduced against a running server and checked against the specification text); medium (one of the two); low (inferred, or the specification is ambiguous)
                Client impact handled, degraded, blocked, none

                The distinction between undeclared and declared-not-working is deliberate. Both are invisible to a client that trusts the conformance declaration, and conformance tests detect neither.

                Citations to 18-062r2 are checked by tooling, so a requirement, clause or figure that does not exist fails the build. We added this check after a review on 23 September 2026 found four wrong references in five findings. Every claim in a finding is marked as observed or inferred.

                D.6 5. Results

                Results are grouped by theme rather than by service. Each theme names the services where a behaviour was seen. Finding numbers are in brackets, and Annex A lists every finding.

                D.6.1 5.1 Overview

                ZOO-Project-DRU 3 – 2 1 –
                Roofer Online 1 1 – – –
                All filed findings 72 7 42 17 6

                13 findings cover both pygeoapi and ZOO-Project, so the rows add up to more than 72.

                The counts reflect how much each service was tested, not how well it performs. The two team-run implementations were tested continuously from August. The third-party services were tested at the plugfests and whenever they were available.

                D.6.2 5.2 Interoperability matrix

                The matrix summarises, for each service, the capabilities the bid names.

                • For execution, dismissal and callbacks, a cell describes what the service did, by whatever route it was reached (a browser, Node, or curl).
                • The CORS row says whether a web page can reach it.
                • Finding numbers are in brackets.

                "Not tested" means what it says. It is not a polite "no": for most third-party services, only what a page could reach was tested (section 7.5).

                A finer matrix is generated from the findings' metadata. It lists about 50 capabilities, from link relations to poll pacing, against each service. It is regenerated whenever a finding changes, and is kept with the finding records; the version used for this report was generated on 13 November 2026.

                D.6.3 5.3 Browser access

                Deployment CORS Location readable by a page Page can name an async job Usable from a web page
                pygeoapi 0.21.0, CORS on yes (0040, 0057) no (0002) no: the 201 body is null (0004, 0039) yes, except asynchronous execution
                pygeoapi 0.21.0, CORS off no – no no (0049)
                ZOO-Project no (0009) – no no (0050)
                ZOO-Project-DRU yes, except on multipart answers (0067) no (0069) yes, from a link in the 201 body yes, with exceptions (0067, 0068)
                Roofer Online no; every preflight answered 405 (0064) – no no
                Varidian registry yes, complete, including Location, Link and Preference-Applied yes yes yes

                Rates.

                • Usable directly from a web page: 3 of 6 deployments.
                • Location readable by a page: 1 of the 3 that allow CORS.
                • An asynchronous job a page can name: 2 of 6.

                Two of the six deployments are configurations of the same server, so these rates are indications, not a survey.

                pygeoapi's hidden Location is a bug in releases 0.18.0 to 0.23.x: the expose-headers setting is passed to the CORS library in a form the library ignores. It is fixed in 0.24.0. We confirmed this in a browser on 0.21.0, 0.24.0 and the development branch.

                D.6.4 5.4 Declarations

                • Under-declared. pygeoapi supports dismissal and the job list, but declares neither (0006).
                • Over-declared.
                  • pygeoapi describes every process with the server's execution modes, not the process's own (0059).
                  • ZOO-Project lists two processes it cannot describe (0020).
                  • It also lists 104 Orfeo Toolbox processes that cannot run (0066), and advertises an input format its build cannot read (0070).
                  • Its asynchronous capacity falls by about one worker per job until no job runs, while it still answers 201 (0044).

                D.6.5 5.5 Inputs

                • The minimal request.
                  • ZOO-Project cannot parse a body carrying only inputs, which is a complete, conformant request (0025).
                  • pygeoapi ignores the outputs block (0028).
                • Validation. Neither team-run implementation validates inputs against the process description (0054).
                  • pygeoapi turns an array sent to a single-valued input into Python text (0031).
                  • ZOO-Project answers invalid or missing input with server errors, error pages or crashes (0016, 0031, 0055, 0062).
                • Inputs by reference.
                  • pygeoapi hands the process the link, unresolved (0058).
                  • ZOO-Project runs the process with an empty input when the fetch fails (0053), and reuses a cached copy indefinitely (0061).
                • Qualified values. pygeoapi hands the process the {value, mediaType} wrapper (0052).
                • Bounding boxes. ZOO-Project discards the CRS sent with a box and relabels it with its own default (0051).

                D.6.6 5.6 Responses

                • Naming a job.
                  • pygeoapi sends Location on every synchronous response, including errors (0024).
                  • Its asynchronous 201 body is null (0004).
                • Response shapes. The default for response in 1.0 is raw, and no implementation follows it fully:
                  • pygeoapi wraps a document answer in a one-element array (0027).
                  • ZOO-Project answers a results document where raw is the default (0072), frames its raw multipart wrongly (0073), returns values where references were asked for (0060), and once answered 200 {} (0071).
                  • ZOO-Project-DRU serves the results of a multi-output job in a form no client can read (0068).
                • Media types.
                  • ZOO-Project labels every raw result application/json (0026).
                  • pygeoapi serves HTML for */* on /results (0036), and labels a JSON dismissal text/html (0037).
                • Results of a failed job. pygeoapi answers 400, as Requirement 46 requires. ZOO-Project answers 200 (0041).

                D.6.7 5.7 Jobs and callbacks

                • pygeoapi never reports a job as running (0032).
                • No team-run implementation sends Retry-After (0033).
                • A failure is described only in prose on both (0034).
                • Both delete a dismissed job, so it can never be read as dismissed (0035).
                • pygeoapi accepts job-list filters and ignores them (0038).
                • Callbacks are declared and honoured by both team-run implementations.
                  • On pygeoapi, an unreachable callback address can stall a job or fail it, and the failure message publishes the callback URL (0047).
                  • On neither does a success or failure callback identify its job (0048).
                  • Callbacks were not checked on the third-party services.

                D.6.8 5.8 Discovery and errors

                • Link relations are written in both the short and the long OGC form (0005, 0011, 0029).
                • Paging. pygeoapi stops the process list at ten entries with no next link (0018). ZOO-Project pages correctly, but with its own parameter (0019).
                • Links built from configuration. ZOO-Project builds every link from a configured root URL. With the shipped configuration, every link leaves the service (0008).
                • Exceptions.
                  • Neither team-run implementation sends RFC 7807 problem documents correctly (0001, 0013).
                  • ZOO-Project answers an unknown path with 400 (0014), and crashes on an unexpected content type (0015).

                D.6.9 5.9 Silent deviations

                In 16 filed findings a server answers with a success status while doing something other than what was asked:

                • pygeoapi: 0018, 0024, 0027, 0028, 0031, 0036, 0038, 0054 and 0058;
                • ZOO-Project: 0041, 0051, 0053, 0054, 0060, 0061, 0071 and 0072.

                0054 applies to both. A client cannot detect these from the response alone.

                D.6.10 5.10 Version 2

                • Roofer Online declares the 2.0 Core class alongside 1.0.
                • The Varidian registry declares a 2.0 process-description class but not 2.0 Core, so the client speaks 1.0 to it.
                • Neither team-run implementation declares 2.0 (pygeoapi including 0.24.0).
                • Weaver, run by the team for this purpose only, is the one server found that declares 2.0 Core (section 3.2).

                D.6.11 5.11 Form generation

                The bid proposed a catalogue of the process schemas that the form generator cannot render. The client builds this catalogue as it is used. When it describes every process of a service (the "process census"), it records, for each input, which JSON Schema keywords the input's schema uses. It also records every input that does not get a control of its own, with the reason. The census was taken on 6 October 2026 in Chromium, against the two team-run implementations, with the client as it then stood.

                Service Processes described Inputs Inline type enum $ref Composition ¹ format contentMediaType Raw JSON fallbacks
                pygeoapi 0.21.0 14 of 14 27 25 1 1 1 6 1 0
                ZOO-Project 701 of 703 ² 5,098 3,662 1,279 0 1,436 1,285 0 0
                1. oneOf, anyOf or allOf. One schema can use several keywords, so the columns do not add up to the number of inputs.
                2. ZOO-Project cannot describe two of the processes it lists (0020).
                • No input fell back to raw JSON. Every input of the 715 processes described got a control of its own.
                • Contradictory schemas. 457 ZOO-Project inputs are booleans whose enum lists the strings "true" and "false", which no value satisfies (0056). The generator follows type, shows a checkbox, and records each case.
                • Axis order. ZOO-Project's echo process accepts a bounding box only in EPSG:4326, whose axis order is latitude first. The client swaps the axes of a box drawn on the map before sending it, and records that it did.
                • Rendered is not accepted. A schema can be rendered and still not describe what the service accepts. ZOO-Project's OGR services declare a GML polygon, but accept one only deep inside a feature collection (0065).

                These counts show that the generator covers the schemas of the two implementations, not that it covers every schema in use. The census was not taken against the third-party services, and the testbed processes on pygeoapi were written by the team (section 9).

                D.7 6. The relay

                D.7.1 6.1 Motivation

                A browser tab cannot do three things a desktop client can. The bid foresaw a small server-side relay to bridge each:

                • Receive callbacks. A tab has no address a server can call. The relay would give each job a token-protected callback URL, and pass notifications to the page over a live connection. Polling would stay the authority on job state.
                • Name asynchronous jobs. Where Location is hidden, as on pygeoapi 0.21.0, the relay can start the job and tell the page its address.
                • Reach services without CORS. For a configured service only, and only after the user confirms, the relay can forward the page's read requests (the "read route"). The direct attempt is always made first and recorded, so the finding that the service is not usable from a browser is kept.

                D.7.2 6.2 Implementation

                The relay is in the repository and tested:

                • per-endpoint configuration;
                • session tokens;
                • a guard against server-side request forgery (SSRF), which refuses private, loopback, link-local and metadata addresses and checks DNS answers and every redirect;
                • rate limits per client and per endpoint;
                • a mark on every response it produces, so its own refusals are never mistaken for the service's;
                • polling reconciliation in the web interface. A callback only prompts the page to poll sooner. The job's state is always taken from the server's own status document. A duplicated, reordered or missed callback therefore changes nothing, and after a relay restart or a reconnect the page polls every unfinished job once, so no change of state is lost.

                D.7.3 6.3 Security considerations

                We decided not to run the relay beside the public web interface. The reasons are security and operational risk:

                • It would be a proxy on our address. Creating a session needs no credentials, so a session token proves only that someone asked for one. Restricting origins limits browsers, but a script ignores it. Rate limits bound how much traffic passes, not who sends it.
                • Third-party operators would see our traffic, not their users'. A public relay sends requests to other organisations' servers from our address. That is acceptable only with each operator's agreement, and the relay's production configuration lists no endpoints.
                • The attack surface would last at least six months. Any server that fetches on behalf of anonymous users is a target for SSRF. Mitigation is in place, but it would have to be maintained for the whole public-demo period.
                • Callbacks are unsafe on pygeoapi. There, an undeliverable callback can stall a job or fail it, and the callback URL is published in the job's message (0047). A relay that is redeployed or briefly down would damage users' jobs.
                • The need fell. By the second plugfest, many testbed services already sent CORS headers.

                D.7.4 6.4 Consequences

                • The hosted interface works with services that send CORS headers.
                • It polls instead of using callbacks.
                • Against pygeoapi before 0.24.0 it cannot run asynchronous jobs.

                The relay stays in the repository. An operator who accepts these responsibilities can run it for their own services.

                D.8 7. Discussion

                D.8.1 7.1 CORS as the gate

                For RQ1 the answer is conditional. A browser client can use a service directly only when the service enables CORS. Asynchronous execution, the mode intended for long computations, also needs one exposed response header. The standard already recommends both (§7.6 Recommendations 4 and 5). Half the deployments tested meet the first, and one meets both. A conformance test run outside a browser cannot observe either.

                D.8.2 7.2 Declarations versus behaviour

                Both team-run implementations differ from their own declarations:

                • pygeoapi under-declares (0006) and over-declares (0059);
                • ZOO-Project lists processes it cannot describe or run (0020, 0066, 0070).

                A client that selects behaviour from the conformance document and process list alone would disable capabilities that work, and offer ones that fail. The client therefore treats declarations as hints, and records what each request actually returned. This is the reason for the undeclared and declared-not-working outcomes.

                D.8.3 7.3 Silent deviations

                The 16 silent deviations share a pattern. The request is accepted and a plausible result is returned. The difference from what was asked is visible only to someone who already knows what to expect. Because neither team-run implementation validates inputs (0054), the checks in the generated form are the only checks a user gets.

                D.8.4 7.4 Divergent implementations

                The raw default (§7.11), the results-document schema (Requirement 32) and the status code of a failed job's results (Requirement 46) are all specified. Yet different implementations answered the same request in different shapes (section 5.6). A client written against one implementation fails against another. A client written against the standard needs tolerances for each.

                D.8.5 7.5 Limited test targets

                The number of services that were fully functional from a browser was too small to serve as a reference. In the absence of such a baseline, observed failures could not readily be attributed to the client, the server or the standard, which made shortcomings harder to locate.

                What "fully functional" means here. A service a page can reach, that runs processes synchronously and asynchronously, and whose results a page can read.

                Of the six deployments, only the Varidian registry met that bar. The others fell short:

                • pygeoapi with CORS on cannot run asynchronous jobs from a page (0039).
                • ZOO-Project-DRU loses CORS on multi-output answers (0067, 0068).
                • ZOO-Project, pygeoapi with CORS off, and Roofer Online cannot be reached at all.
                • Weaver, the only server declaring the 2.0 draft, sends no CORS headers and runs a single process.
                • The services of several testbed parties could not be tested at all.

                Without a fully working service as a baseline, a failure has three possible causes:

                • the client;
                • the server;
                • an ambiguity in the standard.

                Telling them apart took a second implementation and a reading of the specification text, finding by finding. Where both team-run implementations behaved differently, as with the results of a failed job (0041), the standard had to decide which one was right. Where both behaved the same way, as with dismissed jobs (0035), it took a close reading to see that the gap was in the standard.

                Some of the client's own shortcomings stayed hidden for the same reason:

                • Retry-After. A defect in the client's handling of the header went unnoticed, because no server ever sent one (0045).
                • Version 2. The client's browser path has never completed a run against a version 2 server.
                • Callbacks. They could not be checked against any third-party service.
                • Result formats. The client renders only the formats services actually returned, so formats no tested service produced remain unexercised.

                The findings in this report are therefore weighted towards what the two team-run implementations do. A larger set of working testbed services would be the most useful next step, for this client and for any other.

                D.8.6 7.6 Gaps in the standard

                Gap Finding
                On dismissal, clause 13.3 requires 404 for a job that no longer exists, while Security Considerations III.C say a repeat dismissal returns 410. No requirement makes the dismissed status readable. 0035
                Version 1.0 has no machine-readable cause for a failed job: the job status document has no exception member. The version 2 draft adds one. 0034
                Nothing requires a job to be reported as running, or defines what progress must mean. 0032
                Nothing defines what happens when a callback cannot be delivered, and no callback has to identify its job. 0047, 0048

                D.9 8. Recommendations

                D.9.1 8.1 CORS configuration

                A service meant for browser-based viewers should answer every response, errors and multipart answers included, with headers along these lines:

                Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *
Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, DELETE, OPTIONS
Access-Control-Allow-Headers: Content-Type, Accept, Prefer
Access-Control-Expose-Headers: Location, Link, Preference-Applied, Retry-After, Content-Crs, Content-Disposition
                • Answer the preflight. An OPTIONS request must get a 2xx with these headers. Roofer Online's 405 blocks every POST and DELETE (0064).
                • Expose Location. Without it, a browser cannot use asynchronous execution (0002, 0039, 0069). The other headers carry paging, the applied preference, the polling interval, the coordinate system and the file name.
                • pygeoapi operators should run 0.24.0 or later with cors: true. Earlier releases expose no headers at all.
                • Test from a browser. curl and the OGC validator do not apply CORS. A page on another origin, or the hosted interface, does.

                D.9.2 8.2 Access control

                Some services need to know who calls them, to set quotas or to protect data. An API key is the simplest scheme. If one is used:

                • Document it. Put it in the OpenAPI definition, as Requirement 63 asks for access-controlled services [1].
                • Send it in a header, not the query string. Query strings end up in logs and in the addresses of linked resources. The core deliberately does not carry query parameters from one resource to another.
                • Allow the header in CORS. List it in Access-Control-Allow-Headers. An Authorization header must be named explicitly: the * wildcard does not cover it [10].
                • Treat a key in a web page as public. Anyone who opens the page can read it. Use such a key to identify an application and limit its quota, not to protect data. Data that must be protected needs per-user authentication, such as OAuth 2.0 or OpenID Connect.
                • Keep the job list and dismissal access-controlled. 18-062r2 recommends this [1].

                The client does not implement API keys today, because 18-062r2 defines no authentication scheme. If testbed services adopt one, it is a small addition: a per-endpoint key setting in the web interface, kept only in the user's browser. A developer using the core can already send a key through their own fetch.

                D.9.3 8.3 nLDT profile guidance

                The following guidance is proposed to Geonovum for an nLDT profile of OGC API – Processes. Each item raises an existing recommendation to a requirement, restates a requirement that a tested implementation does not meet, or adds a rule the standard does not have.

                1. Raises §7.6 Recommendation 4. A service intended for browser clients SHALL support cross-origin requests, including preflights for POST and DELETE, on every response. (0040, 0049, 0064, 0067)
                2. Raises §7.6 Recommendation 5. A service that supports asynchronous execution SHALL expose Location, and should carry a monitor link in the 201 body. (0002, 0004, 0039, 0069)
                3. Adds. An access-controlled service SHALL use a scheme documented in its OpenAPI definition and sendable from a browser, and SHALL allow its header in CORS. (section 8.2)
                4. Restates Requirements 24 and 49. Inputs SHALL be validated against the process description, and an invalid input SHALL be answered with 400. (0031, 0054, 0016)
                5. Restates Requirements 18 and 24. An input by reference SHALL be resolved before the process runs, and a failed fetch SHALL fail the execution. (0058, 0053)
                6. Restates §7.11. A body carrying only inputs SHALL be accepted. (0025)
                7. Restates Requirements 28 and 32. A document response SHALL be an object keyed by output identifier. */* on /results SHALL return the output's own media type. (0027, 0036)
                8. Restates Requirements 69 and 71. Job-list parameters SHALL be honoured or rejected with 400. (0038)
                9. Restates §7.10. A process description SHALL state the process's own execution and transmission modes, and a listed process SHALL be executable. (0059, 0066)
                10. Adds. Location should be sent only with 201 or 202. (0024)
                11. Adds. A job should be reported as running once it has begun. (0032)
                12. Adds, once the standard is resolved. A dismissed job should remain readable as dismissed for a documented period. (0035)
                13. Adds. Failing to deliver a callback SHALL NOT change a job's status, and callback requests should identify the job. (0047, 0048)
                14. Restates /req/core/process-exception. Exceptions SHALL be RFC 7807 problem documents. (0001, 0013)

                D.9.4 8.4 Client development

                For developers of other clients, the findings suggest the following practices.

                • Treat the conformance declaration as a hint, and check what each request did (0006, 0059).
                • Send Accept explicitly on every request, and parse before trusting a JSON label (0007, 0026, 0036).
                • Follow next links rather than building offset URLs (0018, 0019).
                • Read a job's outcome from its status, not from the status code of /results (0041).
                • Choose the protocol version before sending, and never retry an execution in another version (section 3.2).
                • Pace polling yourself, since no tested server sends Retry-After (0033).

                D.10 9. Limitations and future work

                • Uneven evidence.
                  • Most findings come from the two implementations the team ran, which were tested continuously.
                  • Four filed findings come from services run by others.
                  • The services of several testbed parties could not be tested at all.
                • Process variety.
                  • pygeoapi's processes were written to cover cases, so they show how the framework behaves.
                  • ZOO-Project's shipped catalogue shows how real processes are described.
                • Releases. Later releases may behave differently. pygeoapi 0.24.0 already fixes the hidden Location, and moving the reference server to it is planned. Each finding names the release it was seen on.
                • Version 2. It is supported against the pinned draft, released in 0.6.0, and tested against one real server, from Node only.
                • Browser. Browser behaviour was verified in Chromium only.
                • Untested paths. The client's Retry-After handling has never met a server that sends the header (0033, 0045).
                • Measurement conditions. ZOO-Project's worker-pool decay was measured under emulation on Apple Silicon (0044).
                • Confidence. 16 findings are below high confidence. Medium: 0004, 0005, 0010, 0012, 0016, 0017, 0023, 0030, 0031, 0052, 0056, 0061, 0065, 0070 and 0071. Low: 0043. Each states what would raise it.

                The bid named four risks. Three of them concern the services and the client. The first two occurred; the third did not, in the services tested:

                Risk in the bid Mitigation in the bid Outcome
                Services lack callback support Polling as the baseline; absence reported as a finding Of the services tested, callbacks were declared and working on pygeoapi and ZOO-Project only. The hosted interface polls (section 6.4)
                Services lack CORS headers A proxy as a fallback; the direct attempt is always made and recorded 3 of 6 deployments could not be used from a page (section 5.3). The proxy was built but not deployed (section 6.3)
                The form generator cannot render a schema A raw JSON fallback; failures catalogued No input of the 715 processes described fell back (section 5.11)

                D.11 10. Conclusion

                We built a browser client for OGC API – Processes:

                • a protocol core that other viewers can embed;
                • a web interface that turns any reachable service into forms and map results without code.

                Testing it against six deployments shows that conformance as tested today does not imply usability from a browser:

                • CORS decides whether a page can reach a service at all.
                • An unexposed Location decides whether asynchronous jobs can be used.
                • Declarations and behaviour diverge in both directions.
                • Implementations answer the same request in different shapes.
                • Many deviations are silent.

                A relay could hide some of this, at a security cost we chose not to take on for a public demonstration. The more durable fix is on the service side. Most of the proposed guidance restates requirements or raises existing recommendations, which makes it a modest ask of implementers, and one a profile can check.

                D.12 Availability

                The client is available in three forms:

                What Where
                Web interface https://oap.breinstein.nl
                Source code, MIT licence https://github.com/ITBreinstein/oap-client
                Protocol core on npm https://www.npmjs.com/package/@breinstein/oap-client (@breinstein/oap-client, latest release 0.6.0)
                • Licences. This report is published under the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International licence (CC BY 4.0). The software is published under the MIT licence.
                • Uptake in an app store. The tender asks that the component be ready for uptake in an app store. The installable unit is the npm package. It carries its own type declarations and exports a single entry point, and its README documents the public API: discovery, processes, execution, jobs and versions.

                E. Topic 3: Imagem

                Implement and validate OGC API Processes client

                E.1 Goal

                Implement an OGC API Processes client, make it function within the nLDT ecosystem and have it interoperate with software from other software vendors.

                E.2 Approach

                For IMAGEM, the approach was to continue to build on the progress made in the previous testbeds (2024 & 2025). In these testbeds, first explorations were done to work with OGC API Processes. Where it all started as a static implementation of OGC API Processes, a more complete implementation of the OGC API Processes (v2) was the aim for this testbed.

                In this approach, some new features of the OGC API Processes were tested and implemented in the Digital Twin client (Planspace Simulator). On one side this meant an attempt to create a user interface based on the input and output descriptions of the OGC API Processes provided during this testbed. On the other hand a Job Management System had to be implemented to track the Async processes of this testbed (In previous testbeds, only Sync processes were part of the Adoption topics).

                Going into detail, the approach can be divided in four steps:

                • Creating a catalogue with OGC API Processes
                • Generating UI elements based on the process definition
                • Creating the Job Management System in the client to manage the Async job executions
                • Visualising the results of the OGC API Processes.

                E.3 Client implementation

                The client is the OGC API Explorer in the Planspace Simulator. It is not tied to specific processes: the user selects an OGC API endpoint (Varidian's registry is the default, other endpoints can be added) and the explorer uses the conformance declaration to enable the parts the endpoint supports:

                • Collections (OGC API - Features): browse collections, filter on queryables and add a collection as a layer to the map.
                • Processes (OGC API - Processes): the input form is generated from the process description (JSON Schema), including GeoJSON inputs that can be drawn, uploaded or edited. A process can be executed synchronously or asynchronously, depending on its jobControlOptions.
                • Jobs: asynchronous executions are listed and polled until they finish, with status, progress and timestamps. Jobs can be dismissed when the endpoint supports it, and the results of a finished job can be opened.
                • Results: outputs are rendered read-only from the output schema. GeoJSON, GeoTIFF and feature collection outputs can be added to the map as a layer.

                E.3.1 Try it yourself

                https://nldt.imagem.nl (registration required)

                As part of the deliverables; this client will remain open till at least the 31st of May 2027.

                To use the client:

                1. Register, activate the account and log in.
                2. Create a digital twin.
                3. Open the OGC API tab on the left side of the screen. Here the OGC API endpoints can be explored and processes can be started.

                Figure 1: The OGC API tab with the endpoint selector and the collections, processes and jobs tabs

                Figure 2: An input form generated from the process description (subsidence process)

                Figure 3: The job list with a running and a finished job

                Figure 4: A process result added to the map as a layer

                IMAGEM Testbed 2026 - Plain version

                E.4 Connection with third parties

                Within our application, it is possible to seamlessly switch between OGC API endpoints. We support both v1 and v2 endpoints, as well as endpoints that offer only collections or processes. Here are some examples we worked with during development, in addition to the Varidian Registry:

                E.4.2 ZOO-Project

                ZOO-Project was an existing OGC API that we worked with during the testbed. It had a lot of different inputs and outputs for us to test our implementation with. Some processes had a lot of inputs but were still fully supported by our implementation.

                Figure 7: Example of a process from ZOO-Project

                E.4.3 PDOK

                At the start of the testbed, PDOK happened to release a demo endpoint with a more up-to-date OGC API - Collections implementation. This allowed us to improve our collection filtering system, enabling users to use OGC API - Features as they would use a WFS service in our client.

                Figure 8: Example of the collections available by PDOK

                E.5 Findings

                Inputs and outputs

                Implementing a client with support for every process, including once added in the future, is nearly impossible. Initially the focus was on supporting the given schemas by converting them to JSON schema for automatic form generation. Initially this worked well for our own implementation. Later on however it was noticed that this wasn't really the case for processes from other providers. The main finding here is that the implementation on our side very much dependents on their implementation of the schema. In some cases a very 'minimal' schema was provided; i.e. only requesting an object for GeoJSON inputs instead of requesting an object with its own schema.

                This finding was also noticed on dealing with the outputs. For us as a visualisation client, this is extremely important because without an exact schema of what an output contains, a client is never able to display it properly. As a client we can guess what the outputs would be but that would create work which isn't guaranteed to be needed. Getting the information from the Output parameters is therefore key in supporting the right outputs and reducing the time needed to implement.

                The OGC schema was followed for the process descriptions, inputs and outputs. During the implementation phase it was discovered that a process description could be valid according to the schema but not yet supported by the client. In those cases support for that particular process description was added to the client.

                OGC Schema issues

                The OGC schema / documentation is written in such a way that a party can create an OGC schema compliant API. There is however no such thing for clients! This results in clients implementing only the parts supplied to them by external APIs. This creates a situation where initially only those parts provided by the external API will be supported by the client. This process repeats every time a different external API is connected to the client. A consequence of this is that the client never feels 'ready': it always seems to lack (feature) support for some parts that weren't provided yet. So currently we have a client that lacks feature support until a developer adds support for those 'new' features that were previously missing. This situation was present in basically every process input/output schema.

                Schema might be too complex to fully support in a single client implementation

                Because of the complexity and openness of the OGC schema, implementing an “explorer“ client like we did is “impossible“ without a never ending list of test processes.

                Nevertheless, we are positive about our implementation: it already works with the processes of multiple providers. We do think that a client built for a few specific endpoints or processes, instead of a generic explorer, will result in a better experience for the user. It would also allow visualising a broader range of outputs, without the user needing to style the results themselves.

                Interoperability

                We were able to implement multiple different OGC API providers, both in versions v1 and v2, in the same client without provider-specific code. The differences between providers were handled in a generic way (see Inputs and outputs), not by special cases per provider.

                E.6 Process implementation

                Together with Varidian we developed an OGC API Process called 'Deltares Atlantis Subsidence API'. This is a process we previously maintained as an existing API connection for the calculation model. For the OGC API Process we also simplified and validated the user inputs. The process was originally created by IMAGEM and is hosted in Varidian's registry.

                The process (subsidence_subsidence, "Deltares Atlantis - Subsidence Analysis") performs a settlement calculation for urban development with the Deltares Atlantis calculation module. It is available through Varidian's registry (https://registry.playground.planspace.nl/processes/subsidence_subsidence) and can be run from the OGC API Explorer, synchronously and asynchronously.

                Simplifications compared to our existing subsidence module:

                • The input areas are requested in EPSG:4326 instead of the Dutch RD coordinate reference system, so a client does not need to reproject the coordinates.
                • Multiple levels of dikte (surcharge thickness) can be given in one request, one per feature. This is not possible in our other/existing implementation.

                Inputs:

                • Groundwater level (gw): phreatic groundwater level in m NAP (default -1.0).
                • Surcharge thickness (thickness): fallback thickness of the surcharge in metres (default 1.5, minimum 0.1), used for every area without its own dikte property.
                • Surcharge areas (geojson): a GeoJSON FeatureCollection of polygons, optionally with a per-feature dikte override.

                Outputs:

                • the 'run ID' and three GeoTIFFs (initial subsidence 0-3 years, remaining subsidence 3-60 years, and total subsidence), which can be added to the map as raster layers.

                E.7 Our recommendations for the OGC standard

                • Make the schema of inputs and outputs more specific where a client needs it: a GeoJSON input should state its geometry types, and outputs should always have a schema, so a client can render them.
                • Describe the unit and meaning of inputs and outputs in a machine-readable way, not only in free text. This allows clients to render the proper inputs.
                • Provide more official examples of process descriptions, including inputs and outputs of different types (e.g. GeoJSON, GeoTIFF, units), so client developers know which parts of the schema they should support and how to render them. This should include more examples of geo related processes, such as processes that take and return geometries, features, rasters and coverages.

                E.8 Conclusions

                • A generic OGC API Processes client is feasible: the explorer runs processes of multiple providers, both v1 and v2, with forms generated from the process description and results shown on the map.
                • Its completeness depends on the quality of the process descriptions. With minimal schemas the client cannot show a good form or result, and the openness of the schema means support is never complete.
                • A Job Management System is needed for asynchronous processes, and works with the optional parts the standard gives for it.
                • The subsidence process, created by IMAGEM and hosted in Varidian's registry, runs from the explorer with simpler input than our own module (EPSG:4326, multiple dikte levels).

                F. Topic 4: Collaborall

                DTaaS Testbed 2026, phase 2 · research topic #4 · findings report

                F.1 Summary

                Collaborall investigated how a recipe — a combination of data sources and an ordered list of processes — can be described, found and executed in a national digital-twin App Store, using OGC API Processes, OGC API Records and DCAT-AP-NL. We built the answer on ANT, our existing workflow platform, because that is the realistic case: an organisation adopts these standards on the platform it already runs.

                Our main findings and advice:

                1. A recipe needs its own place in DCAT-AP-NL. It is neither a dataset nor a data service. We propose a small extension — two classes and six properties — that catalogues a recipe beside the datasets and services it uses, so a catalogue can answer which recipes use this service or this dataset. The extension validates against the published DCAT-AP 3.0 and DCAT-AP-NL 3.0 shapes.
                2. A process and a recipe differ in visibility, not in structure. A process is a black box and is catalogued as an ordinary dcat:DataService; a recipe is a transparent composition whose steps can be inspected.
                3. Arazzo is not enough for recipes. It describes a sequence of API calls and nothing else of a workflow. We moved to the Open Workflow Specification (OWS); another awardee independently left Arazzo as well. We advise Geonovum to choose one recipe language and the subset of it a recipe may use.
                4. No standard and no platform carries a value from one step to the next. That wiring is described by the recipe language and has to be executed by whoever runs the recipe.
                5. A catalogue that conforms to the profile can still be rejected by a harvester. The harvester behind data.europa.eu refuses our valid catalogue because of the links that make a recipe a recipe. An extension needs to be tested against real harvesters, not only against shapes.
                6. Several recipes ask for the same plan in different shapes. A shared, published concept set for a scenario — an area and a building programme — would let one input serve many recipes. We built a proof of concept on an existing OGC keyword.

                The recommendations are collected in chapter 9, and a field reference for the recipe extension is in appendix A.

                F.2 1. Introduction

                F.2.1 1.1 The question

                The testbed asked how ready-made solutions for real-world problems can be offered as recipes in an App Store for digital twins. The topic names three mandatory standards — OGC API Processes, OGC API Records and DCAT-AP-NL — and observes that DCAT-AP-NL can describe datasets and services, but not yet recipes.

                We split that into four questions:

                • Describe. Which metadata does a recipe need, and how does it fit DCAT-AP-NL?
                • Find. How are recipes, processes and data published so that they can be searched together, also across the catalogues of several parties?
                • Execute. In which language is a recipe written, and what is needed to run one whose steps are OGC API Processes services?
                • Adopt. What do these standards ask of an existing platform that was not built for them?

                F.2.2 1.2 The use cases

                We worked from questions a municipality asks when it plans housing:

                • What does a housing programme do to an area? A user draws a plan area and enters a programme. The recipe first describes the area from national open data — the BAG and the CBS neighbourhood figures, read live from PDOK — and then projects the programme onto it: the effect on the household and age mix, on the split between price segments, and on the primary schools within reach. Facts and projection are two separate processes, so that the projection can be challenged without doubting the facts.
                • Is there room on the electricity grid? The same area and programme are checked against the national grid-capacity map of Netbeheer Nederland: which feeding areas are affected, what their published congestion status is, and what the development adds to peak demand.
                • Which planned projects collide? Project boundaries are filtered by area and period, and the remaining ones are tested for overlap in space and time. This two-step recipe was our first, on synthetic data.

                Five recipes over five processes are published. Two of them chain more than one step.

                F.2.3 1.3 The approach

                Every part of the solution is exposed through the standards the topic names:

                Deliverable What it is Standard
                Recipe metadata A recipe:Recipe profile on DCAT-AP-NL 3.0 DCAT-AP-NL, SHACL
                Catalogue Recipes, processes and datasets, searchable OGC API Records, DCAT-AP-NL
                Cookbook Each recipe as an executable document Arazzo, Open Workflow Specification
                Processes The steps a recipe calls OGC API Processes
                Client Recepten: find, fill in, execute, show on the map —

                The surface runs on ANT without changes to its core. Each endpoint is a configured endpoint of the platform that returns the document the standard requires, and a small proxy in front supplies the parts of HTTP the platform cannot set itself (chapter 6). The pattern is not specific to ANT.

                F.3 2. Recipe metadata in DCAT-AP-NL

                F.3.1 2.1 Why a recipe is not a dataset

                DCAT-AP-NL 3.0 describes a dcat:Dataset, which is data, and a dcat:DataService, which is an endpoint that serves or processes data. A recipe is neither: it is an executable composition over them.

                A recipe can be catalogued today without any extension, by calling it a dataset with one distribution. We advise against it. It tells every consumer that the resource holds data it does not hold, it brings in mandatory dataset properties that mean nothing for a set of instructions, and — most importantly — it hides what a user searches on: which services does this chain call, and which data does it need? The practical reason to catalogue recipes is to answer which recipes depend on this service and which recipes work on this dataset. A recipe typed as a dataset cannot answer either without the consumer fetching and parsing the recipe itself.

                F.3.2 2.2 The choices

                A recipe is a dcat:Resource with its own class. dcat:Resource is the class DCAT provides for catalogued things that are not datasets. It carries identification, rights and themes without claiming anything about data. A recipe therefore sits in the catalogue beside datasets and services, not disguised as one of them.

                A step points at the service it calls, as a dcat:DataService. This is the choice that matters most. It makes an OGC API Processes endpoint a catalogue entry in its own right rather than a URL hidden inside a document, and it creates the link that answers both search questions above.

                The metadata says that steps depend on each other, not how. We considered typed inputs and outputs per step in the metadata and left them out. The recipe document already states how one step's output feeds the next, and a second copy in the metadata can disagree with it. The metadata records only that a step uses another step's output; the recipe document stays the authority on the wiring.

                The language of the recipe is a metadata value, not part of the model. dct:conformsTo on each distribution names the language the recipe is written in. This was tested by events: when we moved from Arazzo to OWS (chapter 4), the model did not change. Each recipe now has two distributions, one per language, and a client reads dct:conformsTo to choose the one it can run.

                The extension is two classes and six properties. Appendix A lists them with their cardinalities.

                F.3.3 2.3 Process and recipe

                At the first plugfest the participants discussed what distinguishes a process from a recipe. A workflow can contain a workflow, so from the outside every recipe is a process. We therefore draw the line at visibility:

                Process Recipe
                What it is The deepest level a party offers A combination of processes
                Visibility A black box: inputs and outputs only Transparent: its steps can be inspected
                Supplied by Anybody, including other parties Composed from what is in the catalogue
                In DCAT dcat:DataService conforming to OGC API Processes recipe:Recipe

                A process is catalogued as a plain dcat:DataService, not as a class of our own. A class of our own would make every process invisible to a catalogue that does not know our vocabulary. With a plain data service, a recipe can call another party's process, and a harvester that has never heard of recipes still finds the process.

                One further rule follows. The housing recipe reads national open data that we do not publish. Our catalogue does not describe those datasets, because describing a dataset in a catalogue presents the catalogue's publisher as its publisher. The sources are named in the output of each process instead.

                A recipe, as we use the word, combines API processes into one answer and runs in seconds to minutes. When a person has to decide how the work proceeds, the chain becomes an organisational workflow, which depends on how an organisation works rather than on which services exist. That belongs in the organisation's own workflow tooling, which can call a recipe as one of its steps.

                F.3.4 2.4 What is not covered yet

                • Typing of ingredients. The extension has no terms for the object type, units, coordinate reference system or temporal validity of an ingredient. Existing vocabularies are the likely source (qudt:unit, dct:conformsTo, dct:temporal), and we did not want to invent terms that already exist. In practice the client takes its typing from the input schema of the recipe, not from the catalogue.
                • Nesting, execution mode and short human input. For the plugfest we proposed, as a basis for discussion, to let a step be a recipe itself, to state whether a recipe completes within the caller's session, and to model a short input from a person during a run — a confirmation, a choice between two results — as an asynchronous process. P-Plan, a W3C community vocabulary, has terms for plans and nested steps. None of this is built.
                • Other vocabularies. The topic description names DPROD and APKG. We read both and used them as inspiration, but adopted neither.
                • A media type for the recipe document. DCAT-AP requires the format of a distribution to come from an authority list. Neither Arazzo nor OWS is in it, so the distribution can only state application/json.
                • The namespace. Our vocabulary's namespace does not resolve. For this research that made no difference. It matters once several parties publish recipes — see below.

                F.3.5 2.5 One vocabulary, many publishers
